With a product and a visibility restriction written through their canonical paths, replay raised `RuntimeError` and every row and column survived. With no rows, it dropped the two empty tables. A replay that errors, or that removes tables holding nothing, does not destroy, rewrite or invalidate protected state. The earlier edit was reverted. Its broader hand-back of the insurance tables and `issues.class_label` would also have failed condition 3, because it changed the SQL a fresh bootstrap emits.

  **Evidence.** All runs used throwaway `sad_edison_*_test` databases, with the merged revisions taken from a `git archive` of `1cc526049`. `81b7af213` changes neither revision nor any revision before them.
  - Conditions 3 and 4. Each merged and corrected `upgrade()` ran in a rolled-back transaction against two predecessors: the one the merged chain builds, and a historical one holding rows. For `7c3d4e5f6a7b` the historical predecessor has v1 integer-keyed insurance tables holding a policy, an enrollment and a claim. For `6b2c3d4e5f6a` it is an `issues` table still carrying `class_id` and a cached label. Mutating statements, table shapes, per-table row counts and row digests were identical in all four runs. A fresh upgrade through all 152 revisions gives a `pg_dump -s` byte-identical to the merged chain's, checked against both `1cc526049` and `81b7af213`.
  - Condition 5. `tests/dom/core/test_migration_idempotency.py` seeds protected state through canonical paths, re-applies each revision over the migrated schema, and asserts that rows and columns survive. For `7c3d4e5f6a7b` it writes an insurance definition (FEAT-CLASS-003), a purchase (FEAT-OBL-004) and a TRANSACTION claim (FEAT-STOR-003). For `6b2c3d4e5f6a` it files an issue through `create_issue`. Both tests pass against the corrected revisions and were watched failing against the merged ones, with `insurance_policies` and `class_label` gone. No productivity-date row is seeded: a PRODUCTIVITY claim needs a READY Economic Engine, and no FEAT sets `expected_weekly_hours`.
  - Upgrade, downgrade, re-upgrade and heads. There is one head (`b4c5d6e7f8a9`), and both a fresh upgrade and a re-upgrade to head succeed. Downgrade from head cannot reach either revision, on merged or corrected code. `f0a1b2c3d4e5` refuses ("Ledger canonical indexes are not safely reversible"), and `7c3d4e5f6a7b`'s own downgrade raises by design. Each attempt rolled back and left the database at head. No `downgrade()` changed.
  - Corpus lint exits 0 against the unchanged baseline, and both migrations lint clean.

  **Residual behavior.** A re-application over v2 insurance tables that hold no rows, or over an `issues` table with no rows, still drops them. Those states cannot be told apart from the predecessor a fresh chain presents, so condition 3 permits no guard that separates them, and they hold no protected state to lose.

  **Production risk (SOP-DB-015 §IX).** Every deployed database has already applied these revisions, and a normal upgrade never runs them again, so nothing changes for those databases. The risk sits in the paths that do re-run a merged revision: a stamp moved back, a partial re-run, or a manual replay. On those paths the corrected revisions now leave live insurance definitions, claims and frozen issue labels in place, where they used to drop them. A database whose insurance tables or `issues` genuinely held no rows still loses those tables or that column, as before. The PR must carry this acknowledgment.

  The same rolled-back sweep found other revisions that raise when re-applied: `0009a1b2c3d4`, `1761e2187234`, `a3f2c8d91b47`, `2c3d4e5f6a7b`, `e13a59b6aa6b`, `4aa06b69d65d` and `c4b1a9d7e2f0`. They are untouched, because a replay that errors does not qualify under condition 1.

- **The whole test suite stopped collecting once the status identity tests arrived (2026-09-15)** — `tests/test_status_identity.py`, added with #1385, imports `google.auth` at module level. `google-auth` is declared only in `status_service/requirements.txt`, while the Full Test Suite and the Schema Change Gate install `requirements.txt` alone. Their `pytest` runs collect the whole tree, so collection stopped with `ModuleNotFoundError` and no test ran. The Schema Change Gate failed this way on #1387, and the nightly Full Test Suite, last green on `16fecbd87`, would have failed the same way.

  The obvious fix was the wrong one. Installing `status_service/requirements.txt` beside `requirements.txt` resolves `protobuf` down from 7.36.1 to 6.33.6, and that `protobuf` backs the application's OpenTelemetry exporter (`opentelemetry-proto`, `googleapis-common-protos`). The nightly would then test a different telemetry stack from the one production runs. The module now calls `pytest.importorskip("google.auth")` before its status-service imports. Environments built from `requirements.txt` alone report it skipped, with the missing requirements file as the reason, instead of aborting collection. `deploy-status.yml`, which installs both requirement files and names this module explicitly, still runs it.

  Verified locally. With only `requirements.txt` installed, `pytest --collect-only -m "critical or regression" tests/` failed collection before the change. After it, collection is clean and the module skips. In a Python 3.13 environment with both requirement files installed, mirroring `deploy-status.yml`, the module's tests still run and pass.

- **Status deployment now includes its shared projection package (2026-09-14)** — The workflow built `status_service/Dockerfile` with `status_service/` as its context, so the deployed image omitted the sibling `status/` package imported by `status_service.app`. Both Cloud Run services consequently failed to boot with `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'status'`. The image now builds from the repository root and explicitly copies only `status/` and `status_service/`; a workflow regression test holds the context and package-copy contract.

- **The first status deploy verified neither service it deployed (2026-09-15)** — Merging #1385 ran `deploy-status.yml` against production for the first time (run 34928799354). Both Cloud Run revisions went live with the `--update-*` flags, so no configuration was stripped. The job still failed, and in the way that mattered most. Its public health check curled the service's `run.app` URL, but the services run with restricted ingress, so a request from a GitHub runner gets Google's own 404 page, never the Flask app. That check predates #1385 and could never have passed from CI; no deploy had reached it before. The operator auth check sat after it, and steps after a failure are skipped by default, so the deploy that most needed that check skipped it. The operator service's `IAP_AUDIENCE` and allowlist are still unverified.

  The curl is replaced by a check that describes both services and requires, for each:
  - a `Ready` condition of `True`;
  - a latest ready revision equal to the latest created one, which catches a newer revision that failed to start;
  - all traffic routed to that revision;
  - this commit's image.

  It inspects the services rather than requesting them, so ingress does not matter. It does not exercise the application; `gcloud run deploy` has already started the revision and waited for it. The operator deploy step now has an id, and the auth check runs on `!cancelled() && steps.deploy_operator.outcome == 'success'`, which means whenever the operator deploy succeeded, whatever failed in between. `tests/test_status_deploy_workflow.py` now covers that run condition, the absence of any HTTP probe, and the step order. It also runs the readiness script against described services: both serving, not ready, a newer revision that failed, no ready revision, traffic split, traffic on an old revision, and an old image, asserting that the healthy service is not blamed. Against the workflow on `main`, 10 of its 24 cases fail. Each of these turns its own test red: ignoring readiness, a newer revision, traffic or the image; accepting any traffic share; exiting 0 unconditionally; dropping or loosening the auth check's condition; or restoring a curl.

- **Re-applying the Store consolidation migration destroyed Store policy state and severed entitlements (2026-09-14)** — Corrected the replay guards in historical migration `b7c41e9a2f30`, as a Replay-Safety Correction under `SOP-DB-011` §V.A. Its three destructive steps were keyed on bare existence, and `store_products` names both the legacy JSON-payload table the revision replaces and the versioned table it creates. Re-executed against its own target schema, it dropped the versioned `store_products` table and recreated it empty in its 2026-09-07 shape, cleared `store_item_visibility`, and NULLed every `entitlement_events.product_id`. The correction preserves first-application behavior and makes replay non-destructive. `test_store_products_consolidation_is_idempotent` compared only column sets, so the loss passed unseen until #1369's Store revisions changed the table's shape. After #1369, a replay brought back `tier` and `limit_per_student` and lost `activation_at`, `available_with_overdue_obligations`, `direct_purchase_allowed`, `economic_role` and `holding_limit`.

  Governing authority: `INV-CORE-000` §II places all migrations in its scope, its §III invariants are cumulative, and `INV-CORE-001` §III ranks `INV` and `DOM` above procedure. The replay destroyed state those documents protect:
  - Store policy versions. `DOM-POL-001` §VI.0 assigns each row an immutable `policy_uuid` that is never rewritten. §VI.1 makes Policies an append-only, immutable repository whose prior rows remain readable for provenance. `INV-ARC-016` §IX prohibits direct mutation of immutable historical policy versions.
  - Per-product visibility, which `DOM-CORE-002` §V.2 and §V.11 place among the Policies repository's definition tables.
  - Entitlement facts. `DOM-STORE-001` §VII.A makes `entitlement_events` append-only canonical history, one row per immutable fact.

  No forward migration can change what this revision does when it is itself re-run.

  Each step now acts only on the legacy shape, keyed on a marker that no later revision removes. The table drop requires `store_products` to lack `product_lineage_uuid`. The visibility clear requires `store_item_id`. The `product_id` clear and retype require the column to still be an integer.

  Evidence, per `INV-ARC-017` §V–§VI, all against throwaway `*_test` databases:
  - First execution is unchanged (§V.A conditions 3 and 4). Replayed to predecessor `97e131ddb211` with legacy rows seeded, the merged and corrected `upgrade()` emit the same 20 mutating statements and leave the same schema and row counts. A fresh upgrade through the full chain yields a `pg_dump -s` byte-identical to `main`.
  - Replay is non-destructive (condition 5). The consolidation test writes a product through `publish_store_product`, a visibility restriction through `set_product_visibility`, and a grant through `execute_direct_grant` (FEAT-STOR-004). It then re-applies the revision and asserts all three survive before comparing columns. It fails on the lost data against the merged revision and passes against the corrected one. A rolled-back re-application with every statement captured shows no destructive statement.
  - A downgrade to `97e131ddb211` followed by re-upgrade matches the fresh schema up to column order. There is one head, and `scripts/lint_migrations.py` passes for the file and against the baseline.

  Production risk acknowledgment, under `SOP-DB-015` §IX: a database already at or past `b7c41e9a2f30` never executes it, so the correction cannot change it. A canonical database before it executes the same statements as before. The residual risk is a database whose Store tables were altered by hand into a non-canonical intermediate: `store_products` given `product_lineage_uuid` while still in legacy shape, `product_id` retyped by hand, or visibility rows carrying both keys. There the corrected revision skips a step the merged revision would have run, and the outcomes diverge. The mitigation is `SOP-DEP-023` §VI, which requires a verified backup and confirmed head and target state before upgrade. Any such database needs operator review before upgrading.

  `e0f1a2b3c4d5` was evaluated under §V.A on its own evidence and does not qualify. Re-applied after `b4c5d6e7f8a9`'s rename, it adds a stale `essential_when_overdue` column beside `available_with_overdue_obligations`. That is drift. It destroys, rewrites or invalidates no protected state, so it fails condition 1, and it remains unedited.

  The same rolled-back sweep of all 152 revisions found replays outside the Store set that drop live tables or columns: `7c3d4e5f6a7b` (insurance tables), `0001_bootstrap` (Store tables) and `6b2c3d4e5f6a` (`issues.class_label`). Each is to be evaluated independently under §V.A and is not corrected here.

- **Store surface regression test still required the retired pricing tier (2026-09-14)** — `tests/dom/class/test_store_surface_no_rent_panel.py` asserted `#tier-recommendation` as the legitimate Store guidance, and #1369 retired the tier for the economic role (SPEC-STORE-001 v1.1). Its "no rent `#cwi-info`, no `displayCWIInfo`" half still held. The "guidance is preserved" half now targets what SPEC-ECON-003 requires. For the unified CWI Helper (§4.9), that is the price input's `data-economy-validate="store_item"` feeding `#economy-warnings`. For the economic-role reference band (§4.7), it is the `data-economic-role-select` control driving `#role-recommendation`. It also asserts the tier container stays gone, and that `#economy-data`, `economy-balance.js` and `item-form-economy.js` are present, since without them the markup binds nothing. The Edit Item check used to read `admin_edit_item.html`, which now holds only the shared macro call, so the test renders the real edit route instead. A review of #1369 flagged the edit page as missing the economy hooks. The rendered page confirms the shared macro provides them. Removing `#economy-warnings` from the macro, the role-select wiring from the script, or `economy-balance.js` from the edit page each turns the relevant test red.

- **Constitutional CI failed every Dependabot PR before running any evidence (2026-09-14)** — The evidence runner step set `SECRET_KEY`, `ENCRYPTION_KEY` and `PEPPER_KEY` from `${{ secrets.* }}`. A `pull_request` run triggered by Dependabot receives only Dependabot secrets. Binding `environment: testing` does not change that, and this repository has none. Each reference therefore expanded to an empty string. `tests/conftest.py` fills in `ENCRYPTION_KEY` with `os.environ.setdefault()`, which does nothing when the variable is already set, even to an empty string. So `app/__init__.py` raised `Missing required environment variables: ENCRYPTION_KEY` while loading conftest, and every selected evidence family failed. All 13 Dependabot PRs opened on 2026-09-14 (#1370–#1382) were red for this reason alone, e.g. run 34826796144 (`Secret source: Dependabot`). Fork PRs would have failed the same way. Human PRs passed only because their secrets happened to be non-empty. The result depended on who opened the PR, not on what it changed.

  The secrets did no useful work even on human PRs. Every evidence command is a pytest module under `tests/`, so conftest loads first. It hard-sets `SECRET_KEY` and `PEPPER_KEY`, and it supplies `ENCRYPTION_KEY` and `AUDIT_HMAC_KEY` when they are unset. The four auxiliary validators the runner can start use only the standard library and never import the app. The step now passes no key variables, as `full-suite.yml` already did.

  `accessibility-gate.yml` and `schema-gate.yml` repeated the pattern with all four keys. Their `flask db` steps import the app outside pytest, where conftest cannot help, so those steps now carry explicit throwaway values, the same ones conftest uses, instead of secrets. Their pytest steps pass no keys. Neither gate had failed yet only because Dependabot's `requirements.txt` and workflow bumps fall outside their `paths` filters. None of the three jobs reads application keys from Actions secrets any more, or anything from the `testing` environment, and no ruleset requires a `testing` deployment. `environment: testing` is therefore removed from all three, which also stops each PR push from recording up to three deployments. The `testing` environment's four secrets are now unreferenced by any workflow.

- **Replayed Ledger commands are compared under the fingerprint version they were accepted with (2026-09-14)** — Two defects in this branch's idempotency changes, raised in review. Adding `correlation_id` to the replay fingerprint changed the digest of every command, null or not, without a new `FINGERPRINT_VERSION`, so a retry of any reservation accepted before deployment would have failed closed as a mismatch. For monthly savings interest, whose reservation is replayed hourly for the rest of the month, that was a certainty. And the change that let interest survive a recomputed amount switched off fingerprint *and* version comparison for every single-effect `Interest` command, so a key reused for another seat or account would have replayed that seat's transaction instead of refusing.

  `SPEC-LED-002` §4.1 excludes per-request values from the fingerprint, so `correlation_id` is out of it again; it is still persisted on the transaction, which is what issue reversals needed it for, and `original_transaction_id` already pins the one caller that carries a prior correlation forward. Every non-Interest digest is byte-identical to `main`. §4.1 also requires excluding values that legitimately change across retries, and §4.2 makes that a serializer change with a new version: `FINGERPRINT_VERSION` is now 2, identical to 1 except that an Interest amount is encoded as null. A replay is serialized under the version its reservation was accepted with, the stored digest is never recomputed, and an unknown version fails closed (§6.3). The bypass is gone. Transfers and the legacy helper use the same version-aware comparison; the legacy helper's integrity-race path previously compared the digest and ignored the version.

  Known limitation, deliberately not worked around: an Interest reservation accepted under version 1 still includes its amount, so a pre-deployment month replays only while the recomputed amount is unchanged — exactly as on `main`. Accepting it otherwise would mean re-deriving a historical digest, which §4.2 forbids. Covered by golden-digest, correlation, collision, recomputed-amount and version-1 replay tests in `tests/dom/ledger/test_transaction_idempotency.py`; the collision tests were watched failing with the bypass restored.

- **The Store holding limit binds every acquisition source (2026-09-14)** — Only the purchase FEAT consulted `holding_limit`. This branch had removed the direct-grant FEAT's old limit check without replacing it, and rent perks never checked at all, so a teacher grant or a rent payment could leave a student holding more than the product allows. `DOM-STORE-001` §VIII.A makes the limit source-independent, `DOM-OBL-001` and `FEAT-OBL-003` §IV.4 apply it to rent grants by name, and `SPEC-STORE-001` §V.A requires `on_hand + grant_quantity ≤ holding_limit` for any lawful grant.

  `entitlement_service.ensure_within_holding_limit` is now the one check, counting active holdings of the lineage from every source (a `GRANTED` lineage with no terminal event) and refusing an over-limit grant whole rather than trimming it; `PRIVILEGE` is hard-set to one. The purchase FEAT and FEAT-STOR-004 call it under their seat lock, and `grant_hall_passes` / `grant_store_entitlements` apply it to the exact product version being granted. A rent perk refused by the limit is its own outcome: rent stays satisfied, the perk is not issued, and the refusal is logged (`FEAT-OBL-003` §IV.4). Covered by `tests/test_store_holding_limit.py` — teacher grant, purchase and rent perk each against the same limit, active rather than lifetime counting, and seat isolation.

- **Overdue rent no longer waves through rent-linked products, and the permission carries its SPEC name (2026-09-14)** — While rent was overdue the purchase FEAT allowed any product linked as a rent benefit, and the form only offered the "allow purchase when rent is overdue" flag on rent-linked items — so the flag could never change an outcome. `SPEC-STORE-001` §IV.D carries overdue-purchase permission solely on the product flag, and `FEAT-OBL-003` §IV.4 forbids reusing the overdue direct-purchase policy for a rent grant, which reaches students through its own path. The linkage bypass is gone.

  The field was introduced as `essential_when_overdue`, a name `SPEC-STORE-001` does not declare (§III.A). The model, form, resolver, publication seam and FEAT now use `available_with_overdue_obligations`, and migration `b4c5d6e7f8a9` renames the column in place with values preserved. §IV.C does not tie the permission to rent linkage, so the form now offers it on any directly purchasable item — collective goals included — whenever the class blocks late purchases; the teacher-facing "Essential" label is unchanged. `SPEC-ECON-003` cited `DOM-STORE-001` §XII for this rule, which says nothing about it; the citation now points at `SPEC-STORE-001` §IV.D. Not addressed here: the SPEC's three-valued class policy (`ALL_ITEMS` / `SPECIFIED_ITEMS_ONLY` / `NO_ITEMS`) is still the boolean `prevent_purchase_when_late`. Covered by `tests/test_store_overdue_purchase_gate.py`, whose fixture asserts rent really is overdue so the block cannot pass by coincidence, and by contract tests in `tests/test_store_item_type_field_gating.py`.

- **Rent can grant privilege items, and every rent perk expires at the rent boundary (2026-09-14)** — The form offered a rent link on privilege items, but the save path's own item-type map had no privilege entry and refused with "'privilege' items cannot be granted for paying rent." The map is now derived from `store_service._RENT_LINKABLE_ITEM_TYPES` and the resolver's single item-type mapping, and the form contract imports that same set, so the three can no longer disagree. Enabling privilege grants exposed a second gap: only hall-pass perks were expired at a rent boundary, while `DOM-OBL-001` §IX.9 and `DOM-STORE-001` expire every rent perk and `SPEC-STORE-001` §V.A says privilege rent grants end at the cycle boundary. `expire_rent_hall_passes` is now `expire_rent_perks` and expires every `PERK` grant of the prior cycle, whatever its type. Covered by `tests/test_entitlement_service.py::test_expire_rent_perks_covers_every_rent_grantable_type` and a parity test that every rent-linkable type maps to a rent grant.

- **Store item forms: grant-only prices, start and delist dates, long-term goals, stale fields, and live price feedback (2026-09-14)** — Six defects on the reworked Store forms, all introduced on this branch.
  - *Grant-only products crashed both catalogs.* A grant-only product stores no price, and `/student/shop` and the admin catalog formatted it with `%.2f`. `SPEC-STORE-001` still declared `price` required, so it is amended to v1.3: required when `direct_purchase_allowed` is true, null for a grant-only product, which is not purchasable and is never offered in the student catalog. The shop query excludes it, the admin surfaces say "Grant only", and migration `d9e0f1a2b3c4`'s downgrade now refuses while any null price exists instead of failing inside `ALTER TABLE` — backfilling one would turn a grant-only product purchasable once the previous revision drops `direct_purchase_allowed`.
  - *A future start date hid the product for ever.* Publishing with a start date wrote the version `HIDDEN`, and nothing ever un-hides one. `SPEC-STORE-001` §IV.C defines `activation_at` as a gate on an `IN_USE` version, so products now publish `IN_USE` and the date is enforced at read time — by the resolver's `is_purchasable` and by the shop query. A collective goal's instance code is issued at publication, since no later step would issue it.
  - *Editing erased the delist date.* `auto_delist_date` had been dropped from the form and the translator hard-coded `None`, so any edit of a scheduled product silently removed its date. It is back, offered wherever an item is directly purchasable; delisting ends sales only and never terminates a held entitlement (§V.A). Restoring it exposed a prefill that could not round-trip: the date is stored as the exclusive end of the chosen day (the next midnight in the system-level authority, UTC), and the edit form read it back with `.date()`, which depends on the database session's timezone. On a UTC session it showed the following day, and saving persisted it, so each edit moved the delist date — and a collective goal's deadline, which uses the same conversion — a day later. The form now steps back a microsecond and reads the day through the canonical temporal resolver, and reads a start date back in UTC, where it was written. The regression test seeds the value exactly as the form writes it; it passed on a Pacific-time local database by coincidence and was watched failing under `PGTZ=UTC`, which is what CI exposed.
  - *Every browser save marked the item a long-term goal.* `is_long_term_goal` was rendered as a hidden input, and WTForms renders a `BooleanField`'s hidden value as `y`, so it always submitted true and excluded the item from CWI checks. It is a checkbox again, offered wherever a price is.
  - *Hidden controls still submitted stale values.* The gating script only hid illegal controls, so a bundle left checked before switching to an immediate item was posted and refused as inapplicable. Hidden controls are now disabled, and cleared when a teacher's change makes them illegal. On its own that was not enough on the edit form: the POST form was built from the stored item, so a field the browser did not send fell back to its stored value and was refused anyway. The route now seeds from the item on GET only.
  - *Live CWI price feedback bound nothing.* The contract renderer was copied into both templates, and the copies lost the price input's `data-economy-validate` marker and the `#economy-warnings` container. Both forms now render through one macro, `templates/macros/store_item_form.html`.

  A review claim that every non-collective price field was labelled "Goal Amount" was checked and not reproduced: the form renders the WTForms label. Covered by `tests/test_admin_store_edit_route.py`, `tests/test_student_shop_render.py` and `tests/test_store_item_type_field_gating.py`, and in a real browser by `tests/test_store_item_type_gating_browser.py`, which loads the rendered form with the actual gating script in Chromium and drives every reachable contract state: the fields shown are exactly the contract's, hidden controls are disabled and absent from the submitted `FormData`, a value made illegal by a type change is cleared, a dependency toggle hides without discarding, and the edit form keeps persisted values. All four tests were watched failing against the hide-only script this branch first shipped. They skip where Playwright or Chromium is unavailable, like the axe audit.

- **Every economy rebalance row reaches the command that owns it (2026-09-14)** — `FEAT-CLASS-005` §XI delegates rent to rent supersession, Store prices to product-version supersession and the overdraft fee to Economic Engine evolution. Queuing skipped any change whose type had no domain, so a scheduled Store price queued nothing, and `_apply_change_list` had no overdraft branch, so an immediate overdraft fee changed nothing — while both paths flashed success. A scheduled late penalty also carried no activation date and so activated on the next sweep, and rent and its late penalty shared a conflict key, so queuing both silently cancelled the rent change.

  An unmapped change type is now refused rather than skipped. Store prices and the overdraft fee have no later activation boundary (`FEAT-ECON-001` §VIII), so they are immediate-only and a scheduled submission containing them is refused with an explanation; applied immediately, each records an applied policy transition. The overdraft fee goes through the Economic Engine evolution command, composed inside the rebalance's existing `FEAT-CLASS-005` context because nested FEAT contexts are forbidden. A late penalty activates at the same rent boundary as rent, and the conflict key includes the change type. Covered by `tests/test_economy_rebalance_owning_commands.py`.

- **Issue resolution reverses or refunds only an unused Store purchase (2026-09-14)** — "Reverse and remove item" and "Refund and retain item" were offered for any transaction a student reported, and the route never checked what kind of transaction it was. Refunding a payroll credit posted a negative reversal and debited the student. `SPEC-OPS-001` §3.7 makes reversal available only where the governing domain authorizes it — absence of a prohibition is not authorization — and the authorization that exists is narrow: `FEAT-LED-002` §I resolves an eligible pre-use Store purchase, and `DOM-SUP-001` §IX offers the two outcomes only for an unused or pending item.

  `ledger_correction_service.resolve_purchase_resolution_eligibility` now decides both what the page offers and what the route accepts: a Store purchase, not already reversed, with no obligation provenance, whose grants to the buying seat are all still active, and not insurance coverage. Anything else is offered a manual credit, with the reason shown. Revocation now covers only the buying seat's grants; it previously matched every grant sharing the correlation. The existing reversal test reversed a hand-built deposit — exactly the case the specs forbid — and now uses a real purchase. Covered by `tests/dom/interpretation/test_issue_resolution_reverse_transaction.py`, including a payroll issue that offers neither outcome and leaves the balance untouched, and a used item that is refused.

- **The current rent policy is the newest recorded version (2026-09-14)** — `get_rent_settings` ordered by `rent_effective_at` before configuration time, and the Store's rent-link save passed the product's start date as that effective date. Future-dated terms were therefore read as current by every operational caller: the overdue purchase gate, cycle creation and legacy rent payment. A deferred economic change is a policy transition activated at an operational boundary (`FEAT-ECON-001` §VII–VIII), and a cycle already underway keeps the `policy_uuid` it froze, so a superseding version is in force the moment it is recorded. `supersede_rent_settings` no longer accepts an effective date, and the reader orders by recording time again. Covered by `tests/test_rent_settings_current_reader.py`.

- **A compensating transaction keeps the original's correlation on every path, and a reversal cannot be reversed (2026-09-14)** — `reverse_transaction` left the compensating row's correlation unset unless the caller opted in, and `Transaction.before_insert` then stamped the active FEAT's correlation. `FEAT-LED-002` §II.2 requires the correlation to inherit or extend the original, and `SPEC-OPS-001` §3.1A requires `REVERSE` and `REFUND` to share it; a scheduled collective-goal refund instead recorded an unrelated operation's correlation. The opt-in is gone and the row always inherits. The insert-time integrity hook had accepted a compensating row in its mixed-correlation check but still rejected it in the Tier-1 correlation check, so inheriting under a Ledger FEAT would have raised. It now decides once whether a row is a compensating effect of the transaction it names and applies that to both checks, and a compensating row no longer overwrites the session's active correlation. Separately, a row whose type is `REVERSAL` carries no reversal link of its own, so nothing stopped it being reversed in turn; `SPEC-OPS-001` §3.6 makes a reversal terminal, and `reverse_transaction` now refuses one. Covered by two tests in `tests/dom/ledger/test_reversal_ownership_and_deletion.py`.

- **Budget survival no longer fails when a class has insurance, and insurance advisory rows are judged in the class's policy mode (2026-09-14)** — This branch started passing the class's insurance policy versions into the economy analysis, and the budget survival check read `premium` and `charge_frequency` as attributes of `PolicyVersion` rows, which carry their terms in the payload — so any class with an active policy and a configured CWI raised `AttributeError`. It now reads active versions' payloads and skips a version with no usable premium. On the rebalance review, an advisory insurance row has no change, and the route indexed `item['change']` while matching the teacher's selection, so any selected row listed after one returned 500. The insurance row also resolved its band with `checker.mode`, an attribute that does not exist, so tight and comfortable classes were always judged against the default band; it reads `policy_mode`. Covered by `tests/test_economy_rebalance_owning_commands.py`.

- **Redemption prompts follow `SPEC-STORE-001`, and purchased privileges always need an expiry (2026-09-14)** — The publication seam and form accepted a redemption prompt on collective goals and refused it on hall passes, while `SPEC-STORE-001` §IV.C allows it on delayed-use and hall-pass products only. One set, `store_service._PROMPTABLE_ITEM_TYPES`, now drives the seam, the form contract, the field rules and the save translation. The privilege expiry rule read the raw `direct_purchase_allowed` key, so a privilege published without that key — directly purchasable by default — needed no expiry. Covered by `tests/test_store_publication_rules.py`.

- **`SPEC-OPS-001` v1.2 scopes grant invalidation to the `REVERSE` outcome (2026-09-14)** — v1.1 added the §3.1A `REFUND` outcome, which retains the entitlement, but left §3.1, §3.3, §8.1 and INV-OPS-002 saying every reversal invalidates derived grants, so an implementation following those sections would revoke an item a teacher chose to leave with the student. They now name the `REFUND` exception and make clear that propagation belongs to the `REVERSE` outcome, not to every Ledger row of type `REVERSAL`. `SPEC-ECON-003`'s insurance section also cited §4.5.6, which does not exist, and §4.5 (Fines) as the insurance scope boundary; they now cite §4.4.3 and §4.4.

- **Accessibility: rebalance controls, issue resolution and class deletion (2026-09-14)** — On the Economic Engine rebalance review, the per-row midpoint and custom radios had labels with no `for` and inputs with no `id`, the custom amount was labelled only by its placeholder, and the effective-date radio group's heading was a bare `<label>` associated with nothing (`INV-ARC-020` §VII.C). Each row's choice is now a `fieldset` with a `legend` naming the row, every radio has an associated label, the amount has a visually hidden label and is described by its help text, and the effective-date group is a `fieldset`. A static inline `max-width` was removed (`SPEC-DES-001` §IX.2). On the issue page, when a transaction cannot be reversed or refunded, the reason is attached to the action select through `aria-describedby`. The class-deletion gate's "will not deleted" now reads "will not be deleted". `pytest tests/test_accessibility.py tests/test_axe_compliance.py` passes.

- **Saving a store item did nothing, and said nothing (2026-09-13)** — Editing an item's price through `/admin/store/edit/<product_lineage_uuid>` re-rendered the form unchanged. Creating an item was broken the same way. Two defects compounded.

  `StoreItemForm.validate()` checks every submitted field against the form contract's `legal_fields` and rejects anything outside it, which is the right rule applied to the wrong set: it walked `self._fields`, which includes WTForms plumbing. `legal_fields` is derived from the contract's item-configuration sections, so `submit` and `csrf_token` can never appear in it. A browser sends both on every POST, so every real save was refused with "This field is not applicable to the selected item configuration" against the submit button. The guard now skips `SubmitField` and `CSRFTokenField` and is otherwise unchanged — an illegal configuration field is still caught.

  The reason this reached a teacher is the second defect: neither `templates/admin_edit_item.html` nor `templates/admin_store.html` rendered form errors at all. `StoreServiceError` was flashed, so a service-layer refusal was visible, but a validation refusal produced a blank re-render indistinguishable from a no-op. Both templates now render per-field errors inline and a form-level summary, the latter because errors on unrendered fields — exactly this case — otherwise have nowhere to appear.

  Covered by `tests/test_admin_store_edit_route.py::test_admin_store_edit_post_changes_the_price`, which posts `submit` as a browser does. No prior test did, which is why a form that rejected every browser submission had a green suite. It was watched failing with the `forms.py` fix removed.

- **Collective goals were bought for free and could not be refunded when they expired (2026-09-13)** — `execute_store_purchase` returned early for `COLLECTIVE_GOAL`, writing the `GRANTED` entitlement event and skipping the Ledger entirely, so participation cost nothing. `collective_goal_expiry_feat` then failed with `sqlalchemy.exc.NoResultFound` on every unmet goal, because it looked for the purchase transaction that the early return never wrote — a class whose goal lapsed could not be unwound.

  The specs decide against the early return on both counts, and the comment justifying it cited them inverted. `DOM-STORE-001` §VIII.E.5 requires an expiring goal to coordinate a lawful refund, which presupposes a payment. `FEAT-STOR-001` §VII.E puts goals on the ordinary purchase path, and §XIV prohibits special-casing them into a separate entitlement-persistence path by name. The early return is gone; goals debit and refund like any other purchase.

  The same premise had been written into the publish seam, where `_validate_definition` both forbade a collective goal from carrying a price and exempted it from the "directly purchasable items require a price" rule. That was already contradicted by the form contract, which renders `price` as a required "Goal Amount" for collective goals — so a teacher could set a goal amount the catalog then refused to store. Both exemptions are removed.

  `tests/test_collective_goal_expiry.py` goes from 4 failed to 6 passed; the store and entitlement suites pass at 120. Four test fixtures that hard-coded `price=None` for collective goals were corrected, including two assertions that a purchase left the buyer's balance untouched.

- **Economy analysis 500'd for any class with a store product (2026-09-12)** — `POST /admin/api/economy/analyze` raised `'StoreProduct' object has no attribute 'is_active'`. `EconomyBalanceChecker.check_store_items_balance` re-filtered the products it was handed on a v1 `is_active` column; the catalog carries `availability_state` now. Any class with both `expected_weekly_hours` configured and one product in the catalog lost the whole analysis — CWI, recommendations and all.

  Availability is the caller's decision, not the checker's: every call site already resolves the sellable set through `store_service.list_products(..., states=(IN_USE,))`. The re-derivation is gone. The same stale filter was removed from the fines loop and from the cheapest-policy selection in the budget survival test, where `InsurancePolicy` likewise carries `availability_state`. `is_long_term_goal` stays — that is a pricing intent the teacher declares, not an availability state.

  Covered by `tests/test_economy_analysis_store_items.py`; all three cases were watched failing with the filter restored. Note the endpoint case needs `expected_weekly_hours` in the payload — without it CWI is undefined, the analysis returns early, and store pricing is never reached.

- **Store item forms offered configuration the publish seam would reject (2026-09-12)** — Both the Add New Item form and the edit form exposed every configuration group regardless of item type, so a teacher could bundle an immediate-use item or put a bulk discount on a collective goal. `store_service._validate_definition` refuses those per SPEC-STORE-001 §V.A, which meant the entire save failed with no indication of which control was the illegal one.

  `store_service.item_type_field_rules()` now publishes, per canonical item type, which groups are legal — derived from the same frozensets validation enforces, so the editor cannot drift from the seam. Both forms ship the server-resolved contract catalogue, and `static/js/store_item_type_gating.js` selects the contract for the current type and acquisition and hides what it excludes. As first shipped it only hid those controls, so their stale values were still submitted; see the 2026-09-14 Store item forms entry. The create form's hand-rolled collective-card toggle was removed in favor of the shared module; the inner goal-type toggle stays. Also removed the "visible to period/blocks" selector, which had no meaning on a class-scoped page.

  Covered by `tests/test_store_item_type_field_gating.py`; the create-form tagging was watched failing with the attributes and payload removed.

- **Deleting a teacher's last class left a seatless `users` row behind (2026-09-12)** — `POST /admin/join-code/delete` ran FEAT-CLASS-001 unconditionally. Class teardown deletes that class's seats, including the teacher's administrative one, and the orphan sweep deliberately skips the acting principal, so destroying a teacher's only class produced exactly what DOM-IDEN-005 §V.6 calls an invariant violation: a `User` with no `Seat` anywhere. §VI is explicit that teachers get no exception — "Removal of the final classroom SHALL result in destruction of the final administrative `Seat`. If no remaining Seats exist, the corresponding `User` SHALL be destroyed." The confirmation page asserted the opposite in a yellow callout, and the timed gate repeated it.

  The route now dispatches on whether the acting principal retains a seat in any *other* class. If it does, nothing changes. If it does not, the request is terminal account destruction and executes FEAT-IDEN-007 instead, clears the session, and returns a redirect to the login page — one FEAT per request either way. The test is "a seat anywhere else", not "another owned class", because a teacher seated as a student elsewhere still has a seat justifying existence. The confirmation surface, the help panel, and the gate bullets now state the real consequence in each case.

  Covered by `tests/dom/identity/test_teacher_last_class_deletion.py`; the principal-destruction case and the warning-copy case were both watched failing with the dispatch removed. `test_class_delete_clears_the_destroyed_canonical_pointer` was given a second class, since a destroyed principal has no pointers left to clear.

- **A session left open across midnight was closed on the wrong day, and locked the student out of working today (2026-09-11)** — `enforce_daily_limits_job` evaluated every open session against *today's* day boundary, clamping the interval start with `max(active_start, today_start)`. Whenever the scheduler missed a midnight — a stopped dev server, a restart, any outage spanning a day — yesterday's session was re-read as one that began at today's 00:00. Two consequences: the prior day never received its end-of-day row at all, and the closing row that eventually got written was dated **today**, which trips the `done_today` gate in `app/feats/prod.py` and refuses the student's tap-in for a day they had not yet worked. A student active more than twenty hours was tapped out with only the limit recorded.

  The job also implemented DOM-PROD-001 §314 (close at the configured daily limit) while treating §312 (every `active` session terminates by end of day, with the `inactive` row "recorded using the same date as the originating `active` entry") as if it were conditional on that limit — `if not daily_limit: continue` skipped the class outright, so a class with no configured limit was never swept.

  Each open session is now evaluated inside the canonical day of its own `active` row: `evaluation_day_boundaries` is resolved against `latest_event.timestamp` rather than `now`, and the interval end is clamped to that day's close so a session cannot bleed into the following midnight. §312 applies whether or not a limit is configured, and the §314 cap is measured from the real tap-in instead of today's midnight. The job's log line now names which rule closed the session; note that this is a log-only distinction, since both rules write `reason_code = done_for_day` per DOM-PROD-001 and `attendance_sessions` has no free-text reason column. In the persisted record the two rules are told apart by the closure *timestamp*, which is what the tests assert.

  Covered by `tests/dom/prod/test_stale_session_sweep.py`; three of its four tests were watched failing against the pre-fix job, the fourth being a guard that a live session inside its own day is left open.

- **The student policy page invented its purchase date and its coverage start (2026-09-11)** — `student.view_policy` built its `enrollment` view with `purchase_date=utc_now()`, so every policy claimed it was bought at page-load, rendered through a raw `strftime` on a UTC instant — a class in PDT viewing the page after 5pm local read tomorrow's date. Coverage start was read from `ObligationAssessment.coverage_start_time`, which is rent-waiver coverage and carries no insurance row, so it was always `NULL` and the template's `{% if coverage_start_date %}` fell through to "In waiting period" for **every** policy, including one configured with a zero-day wait. The page contradicted its own Coverage Details block, which printed "Waiting period: 0 days" two cards below, and it disabled the file-claim button for holders of active coverage.

  Both values now come from the GRANTED entitlement event, which is what actually records the purchase. Coverage start is computed by `coverage_effective_start_utc` in `app/feats/insurance_claim_feat.py` — the same calendar-day arithmetic the claim-submission gate enforces, so the page can no longer display a start the enforcement path would reject. It was already the helper behind the admin claim-review screen; it is now public rather than module-private. The template renders dates through the `fmt_date` filter (SPEC-TIME-001 / MAP-UI-002), and "in waiting period" is now stated as a future effective date rather than as the absence of data.

  While there, the claims table read `claim.claimed_dates` and `claim.submitted_at`, neither of which exists on the row the route builds — the first policy with a filed claim would have raised on `Undefined.strftime`. It now reads `incident_date` and `filed_date`.

  Covered by `tests/test_student_policy_view_route.py`, all three watched failing against the pre-fix route and template.

- **Six `var()` references pointed at tokens that do not exist (2026-09-11)** — `--student-primary`, `--shadow`, `--text-light`, `--text-dark`, `--brand-primary`, `--brand-blue`, `--brand-teal` and `--brand-gold` were referenced without fallbacks in `style.css`, `error_400.html`, `admin_attendance_log.html` and `system_admin_network_activity.html`. An unresolvable `var()` with no fallback makes the declaration invalid at computed-value time, so the property was silently dropped and the element inherited — the `.bg-student` / `.text-student` / `.border-student` utilities applied no color at all, and the sysadmin network-activity stat numbers rendered as body text. This is the failure mode the token layer is supposed to make impossible, and nothing errored. All eight now resolve to real tokens, verified by a whole-tree sweep that reports zero unresolvable references.

- **`--warning-text` was a token that never existed (2026-09-11)** — `.bg-warning-subtle` read `var(--warning-text, #856404)`, so it had always silently rendered the fallback; the named token was defined nowhere in the repository. It now uses `--alert-warning-text`.

- **Teacher `--bs-secondary-rgb` disagreed with `--bs-secondary` (2026-09-11)** — The RGB triple still decomposed v1's `#d3af37` while the hex token had moved to `#D4A857`. Any `rgba(var(--bs-secondary-rgb), α)` therefore rendered a stale accent, without erroring, and only in translucent contexts. The student and sysadmin themes defined only `--bs-primary-rgb`, leaving the other five to fall through to Bootstrap's blue defaults. All three themes now carry the complete, parity-checked set.

- **`.btn-danger` ignored the role theme (2026-09-11)** — It was the one button rule still on hardcoded `#b3261e`/`#8f1f1f` while every sibling used tokens, so the destructive action was the single control that did not follow the theme. Now `var(--danger)`/`var(--danger-hover)`; verified 5.44:1 against white, unchanged from the value it replaced.

- **A gold stat number on the sysadmin network-activity page failed AA (2026-09-11)** — Sysadmin `--secondary-hover` is `#c09840`, 2.69:1 on white, which fails even the 3:1 large-text threshold — so the figure the page exists to display was the least legible thing on it. Text that must stay legible regardless of role is now pinned in role-agnostic tokens (`--accent-text-on-light` at 6.70:1, and the three `--alert-*-text` values), documented in `SPEC-DES-001` §VI.6. Per-role definitions were rejected deliberately: they reintroduce the same failure, silently, in whichever role picks a bad value. The adjacent `aria-hidden` icon keeps the theme token, as decoration carries no contrast obligation.

  Accessibility validation per `INV-ARC-020` §X: contrast recomputed from resolved values in-browser for all three role themes; `--accent-text-on-light` 6.70:1 and `--text-muted` 7.84:1 (improved from `#5f5f5c`'s 6.41:1) against `--surface`; teacher, student and sysadmin shells confirmed to resolve every token with no unresolved references and no console errors.

- **The student theme paired gold fills with a bronze-era text colour (2026-09-11)** — `--secondary` had converged on gold `#D4A857` in all three roles, but the student theme's companion tokens were never moved with it: `--secondary-text` was still `--text-inverse` and `--secondary-hover` was still the brown `#725132`, and the comment above them literally read "White text on brown". Every shared component that consumes the pair — `.bg-secondary`, `.btn-secondary`, and the badges built on them — therefore rendered white on gold at **2.20:1** in the student shell, and white on `#c09840` at **2.58:1** on hover.

  The teacher theme had the same defect on the hover leg and neither review caught it: `--secondary-text` `#171717` on `--secondary-hover` `#735816` is **2.75:1**. Rather than patch the one reported case, all three roles are now unified on the sysadmin triplet that was already correct — `#D4A857` / `#c09840` / `--neutral-900` — which is the only combination where both the rest and hover legs clear AA. `--secondary-hover` is consequently a *lighter* darkening than a brown would be; that is the constraint, not an oversight, and `SPEC-DES-001` §V.4 now states that the secondary triplet is role-agnostic and why: a role theme MUST NOT set a light `--secondary-text`, and MUST NOT darken `--secondary-hover` past the point where `--secondary-text` still clears AA on it.

  Measured after the change, in the student shell: `--secondary` + `--secondary-text` **8.14:1** (was 2.20), `--secondary-hover` + `--secondary-text` **6.66:1** (was 2.58).

- **Gold was used as body text on fourteen auth pages (2026-09-11)** — Forty `color: var(--secondary)` declarations across the sign-in, sign-up, credential and recovery templates set link, label, subtitle and helper text to the gold *fill*, which is **2.12:1** on `--surface` — below even the 3:1 large-text floor. `--accent-text-on-light` (`#735816`, 6.70:1) exists for exactly this and was simply not reached for. All forty now use it.

  Page-local `<style>` blocks are how the mistake kept recurring, so a floor was added to the shared shell rather than only to the templates named in review: `.auth-body a:not(.btn)` is specificity (0,3,1) and outranks a bare `a` selector, so a page that reaches for the accent again still renders the darkened variant. Buttons are excluded deliberately — `<a class="btn">` carries its own foreground against a filled background, and a bare `.auth-body a` would have turned white button labels dark gold. The `a:hover { opacity: 0.8 }` pattern is also gone: fading `#735816` lands at **4.21:1**, which fails AA on hover only, so hover is now signalled by `text-decoration: underline` at unchanged contrast.

  Accessibility validation per `INV-ARC-020` §X: a per-element audit walked every text-bearing node in `.auth-brand` and `.auth-body`, resolved each element's effective background by climbing ancestors past transparent fills, and applied the 3:1 or 4.5:1 threshold appropriate to its computed size and weight. **Zero failures** across `/student/login`, `/admin/login`, `/sysadmin/login`, `/student/claim-account`, `/admin/recover` and `/admin/signup`, covering all three role shells. `tests/test_accessibility.py` and `tests/test_axe_compliance.py` pass (21 tests).

- **The Turnstile widget was clipped out of the auth card on a phone (2026-09-11)** — Cloudflare's default "normal" widget is a fixed 300px wide and `.auth-container` sets `overflow: hidden`, so on a 320px viewport — where the card is 272px with 224px of content width — the bot check was cut off and the form could not be submitted at all. Widening the shell cannot fix it: reaching 300px of content at 320px leaves roughly 4px of gutter, and Cloudflare's `flexible` size still floors at 300px.

  The four pages that rendered `.cf-turnstile` by hand now call a single `macros/turnstile.html`, which asks for the documented 150px `compact` size below `26rem`. The size has to be set before `api.js` renders the widget; `api.js` is `async defer` in `<head>` and therefore runs after parsing, so the macro's inline script always wins that race. Centralizing it is the point — a fifth page cannot reintroduce the bug by copying a `<div>`, and `SPEC-DES-001` §IX now prohibits hand-written `.cf-turnstile` markup.

  Verified by measuring the shell rather than the widget, since no site key is configured in this environment: at a 320px viewport a 300px element overflows `.auth-container` and a 150px one does not, and the `26rem` threshold is correctly placed — at 417px the media query stops matching and content width is 321px, so the normal widget fits unscaled.

- **The selected page in the docs sidebar was the hardest one to read (2026-09-11)** — `.docs-sidebar .nav-link.active` put gold on the `--primary` fill at `0.9rem`, which is normal text and needs 4.5:1; it measured **4.34:1** in the teacher theme and **3.75:1** in the student theme. Gold is legitimate against a dark fill only where the large-text threshold applies. The label is now `--text-inverse`; the fill already carries the selected state, so nothing is lost. The two remaining gold-on-`--primary` uses in `getting_started_widget.html` were checked and kept: one is an `aria-hidden` icon and the other is bold `h5`, both large text clearing 3:1.

- **Bootstrap hex literals and inline styles survived in the auth templates (2026-09-11)** — The passphrase strength meter in `student_pin_setup.html` carried five hardcoded Bootstrap palette hexes in JavaScript, so the one piece of feedback that tells a student whether their credential is safe was the one piece that ignored the theme. It now uses `--danger` / `--warning` / `--success`; the five zxcvbn steps are still distinguishable because the *bar's width* carries the gradation and the fill only has to say bad / borderline / good. The sign-in wait modal's inline spinner and tip dimensions became `.auth-loading-spinner` / `.auth-loading-tip` on the shared scale, and a stray inline icon `font-size` in `system_admin_login.html` became `.icon-lg`.

- **Submitting the default rebalance activation mode raised instead of scheduling anything (2026-09-11)** — The deferred mode is the form's default and the route's own default, and it could not be submitted. Two independent defects sat on that one path. `apply_economy_rebalance` passed its `FeatureSettings` row into the `class_id` parameter of `queue_scheduled_policy_transitions`, which binds that value into `PolicyVersion.class_id` and `PolicyTransition.class_id`; and the row was fetched with `create=True`, so for any class without one the getter flushed a new `FeatureSettings` outside a FEAT context and `enforce_feat_context_on_flush` raised `FEATContextError` before the first defect could even be reached. Both are gone: the lookup is deleted rather than repaired, because `_apply_rebalance_plan` only ever read `class_id` off the row, and `selected_scope['class_id']` — already authority-checked — carries it. Pinning the rendered radio could not see any of this, so `tests/test_economic_engine_rebalance_activation.py` now drives the POST and asserts a `PolicyTransition` exists; verified failing against both defects.

- **The student dashboard printed a savings balance its own interest projection contradicted (2026-09-11)** — The card shows the *available* balance (posted + pending) while `SPEC-ECON-001` §9.2 accrues on the *posted* balance alone, so between a deposit and the next settlement sweep the page carried a $50.00 card directly above a projection computed on $0.00, with nothing reconciling them. The dashboard now discloses the gap in the same words the transfer page already uses, on the same condition, and states that interest is earned on the posted figure only. Covered by `tests/test_dashboard_savings_projection_disclosure.py`, which also asserts the disclosure disappears once settlement makes the two agree.

- **The delete-gate countdown announced itself as incomplete after completing (2026-09-11)** — `updateCountdownUi` tracked `aria-valuenow` against the bar's width while counting, then set the width to 100% at completion without updating the value, leaving the progressbar announcing its last intermediate percentage. A screen-reader user was told the countdown was still running while the confirmation controls were live (WCAG 2.1.1).

- **Rent was judged against a band boundary the teacher could not type (2026-09-11)** — `check_rent_balance` derived the recommended band as an unrounded product of CWI and the policy ratio, then compared the saved rent against it. At CWI 337.50 the minimum is `$880.5133928571428`, so a rent of `$880.51` was reported as *below* a minimum that prints as the same number — and the next amount the teacher could actually enter, `$880.52`, was the only satisfying one. The Economic Engine showed "Pricing slightly off" for an amount it had itself recommended.

  Money in this application is cents-denominated, so a boundary expressed in fractional cents is not a band the teacher can land on. Both comparison sites now quantize the band to cents before comparing: `check_rent_balance`, and `validate_rent_value`, which had the same shape in a worse form — it compared raw ratios while quoting a 2-dp band back in its own warning text, so the message named a boundary identical to the value it was rejecting. Verified against the reported class: the same `$880.51` now reports `info | Rent is balanced at $880.51/month (2.61x CWI) | min 880.51 max 1100.64`. `tests/test_pricing_recommendation_single_source.py::test_recommended_minimum_is_accepted_as_balanced` covers every policy mode × billing frequency × CWI combination (105 passed) and was proven load-bearing — restoring either old comparison fails 66 of them.

- **One policy ratio produced several different prices (2026-09-11)** — Fixing the band boundary above exposed that the boundary was not the only disagreement: the same policy ratio reached the teacher through four separate derivations, and they did not agree.

  `AVERAGE_WEEKS_PER_MONTH` had three spellings — `economy_policy.AVERAGE_WEEKS_PER_MONTH`, a re-stated literal in `FREQUENCY_WEEK_MULTIPLIERS["monthly"]`, and a float `365.25 / 12 / 7` on `EconomyBalanceChecker` — so a monthly band depended on which module computed it. The Economic Engine card built its monthly band by scaling an already-rounded weekly band, while the rent page scaled the raw ratio and rounded once, which is a different number. `check_rent_balance` converted the teacher's rent to a monthly equivalent before judging it, adding a rounding step the page never took, so a value copied off the page could come back flagged. `validate_fine_value` read the default-mode constants regardless of the class's policy mode, so a tight or comfortable class was shown its own band and judged against another's. And store tiers were matched on the raw ratio while the tiers were printed as cents, so a price typed from a tier's own printed maximum fell a fraction of a cent outside it and was rejected as unaffordable.

  `economy_policy` now owns the month length as a single `Decimal` that the multiplier table and the checker class both reference by identity, plus `weeks_per_period`, `frequency_label` and `scale_band` — the last of which rounds exactly once, because rounding then scaling is not scaling then rounding. `EconomyBalanceChecker` derives every recommendation through `rent_band` / `fine_band` / `store_tier_bands`, the warning and the settings page call the same one, and the warning judges rent in the cadence the teacher set it in rather than in a monthly equivalent nobody sees. `tests/test_pricing_recommendation_single_source.py` covers 3 policy modes × 7 billing frequencies × 5 CWI values (274 passed); before the refactor 136 of them failed.

  The rebalance preview was a fourth derivation, and the one with teeth: `_build_rebalance_preview` scaled `rent_weekly.recommended` — already rounded to cents — into the teacher's cadence through `convert_weekly_amount_to_frequency`, so the amount the "Apply" button would *write* could differ by a cent from the amount the rent page recommends. It now reads `rent_band(...)['recommended']`. Its private `_format_frequency_label` (a third spelling of the cadence wording, which rendered `$880.51 / monthly`) is replaced by `frequency_label`. `convert_weekly_amount_to_frequency` had no remaining caller and is deleted rather than left available, because scaling an already-rounded figure is the defect itself. `test_rebalance_preview_proposes_the_band_the_page_recommends` covers the same 105 combinations and was verified load-bearing: restoring the old scaling fails 45 of them.

  The client was the fifth derivation and the one a teacher actually looked at. `displayCWIInfo` in `static/js/economy-balance.js` took the weekly band out of the analyze payload and multiplied it — `× 2` for biweekly, `÷ 7` for daily, `× value` for custom — so at CWI 999.99 the screen read `$1199.98` where the server's biweekly band is `$1199.99`, and it recovered the "% of CWI" line by dividing the rounded dollar band back out because the ratio was not in the payload. The drift was known: `templates/admin_rent_settings.html` set `suppressRecommendationsEcho: true` with a comment naming "the rounding-drift between the two calculation paths", which hid the contradiction instead of resolving it. The page now asks `/admin/api/economy/validate/rent` for the band and its cadence wording — both from `rent_band` and `frequency_label` — and the policy ratios are exposed on the analyze payload so the percentage is read rather than reverse-engineered. `INV-ARC-022` requires the value shown and the value judged to come from one source; there are no longer two.

- **The student dashboard advertised 4.5% interest the class would never pay (2026-09-11)** — The savings card computed its forecast as `savings_balance * 0.045 / 12` inline. Every part of that was wrong independently: a hardcoded APY inside a projection, which `SPEC-ECON-001` §10 forbids because a projection must use execution's rules; a rate no class configures, where §11 forbids a hidden default APY, so the honest forecast for a default classroom is nothing at all; the *available* balance rather than the posted one accrual actually uses (§9.2); and bespoke arithmetic that ignored the class's calculation type, compounding and payout cadence while labelling the result "/month" regardless of the configured cadence. The transfer page had already been repaired for the same sections and the dashboard was left behind.

  The forecast now comes from `savings_interest_for_payout_period` over `get_posted_balance` with the class's resolved policy, captioned with the real payout cadence, and the whole block is suppressed when no rate is configured rather than inventing one. A dead `apply_savings_interest` wrapper carrying its own `annual_rate=Decimal('0.045')` default is deleted along with its re-export. `tests/test_dashboard_savings_forecast.py` asserts both halves — that an unconfigured class is shown no projection line, and that the rendered figure is the engine's.

- **The rebalance form named a cadence unrelated to when the change lands (2026-09-11)** — Rent is assessed per bill cycle, and a deferred rebalance is scheduled by `_get_rent_effective_at` from the *rent* timeline: the cycle after the upcoming bill. The route's own default is `REBALANCE_ACTIVATION_NEXT_RENEWAL` and its flash already said "the renewal after the upcoming bill". The form nonetheless offered "Next Payroll Run (Recommended)" and submitted `next_payroll` — naming payroll, which has nothing to do with rent assessment. The label now reads "Next Cycle (Recommended)" and submits `next_renewal`, and a `form-text` states the rule the teacher needs: rent takes effect on the cycle after the upcoming bill, store prices change immediately because they are not cycle-based.

  Because that moved submissions out of the one mode the `effective_at is None` fallback in `activate_due_rebalances` covered, the fallback now covers every non-immediate mode — otherwise a deferred transition whose effective date cannot be computed has no later moment to wait for and sits pending forever.

- **The immediate-change warning was shown beside a choice the teacher had not made (2026-09-11)** — The warning that applying a rebalance mid-cycle re-prices rent part-way through it, and its "I understand" confirmation checkbox, rendered unconditionally next to a radio group whose default is deferred. A warning attached to nothing is read as noise, which is how a real one stops being read. Both now live in a `hidden` panel that appears only when "Apply Immediately" is selected, and the confirmation is cleared when the panel hides so a teacher cannot leave a stale acknowledgement behind by changing their mind. `tests/test_economic_engine_rebalance_activation.py` pins the rendered default and the hidden panel; both were confirmed failing against the old markup.

- **An attendance tap raised before it could record anything (2026-09-11)** — `handle_tap` carried `@requires_feat_context("FEAT-PROD-001")` and called `record_attendance_session`, which carries the same decorator. Exactly one FEAT executes per invocation (`INV-ARC-000` §VIII.2), so the inner envelope raised `FEATContextError`. This is the same defect class already fixed in `enforce_daily_limits_job`; the route-level decorator is removed, since the route's own work — PIN verification and resolution — is reads only.

- **"Time Today" was wrong until the first poll (2026-09-11)** — `attendance.js` bootstraps the UI from a server-rendered state blob and then refreshes it every ten seconds. The refresh path already read `duration_today` via `pickTimeToday`, but the bootstrap read `initialState.duration` directly, so a student opening the page saw session duration under a "Time Today" heading for up to ten seconds before it silently corrected itself. Both paths now go through the same accessor.

- **The admin store page raised `NameError` on recent purchases (2026-09-11)** — `derive_display_status` was referenced in `app/routes/admin.py` but never imported. It had also been defined privately in `app/routes/api.py`, so the function a route blueprint needed lived inside another route blueprint; it and its three lineage-query helpers now live in `app/services/entitlement_read_service.py`, where a read-model derivation belongs, and both blueprints import it from there.

- **No ledger effect ever posted, because nothing in the running application settled the ledger (2026-09-11)** — `create_pending_transaction` is the sole ledger write boundary and it creates every effect `PENDING`. `settle_balances` is the only thing that admits an effect to posted history: it assigns `posting_sequence` and advances `LedgerBalanceSnapshot`. Between those two facts sat the defect — no route, service, FEAT or scheduled job ever called settlement. A sweep function existed (`settle_pending_transaction_contexts`, with its own per-context `FEAT-LED-003` boundary and failure isolation), and a CLI wrapper existed (`scripts/settle_pending_transactions.py`), and the sweep's own docstring anticipated "the standalone scheduled sweep". Nothing scheduled it.

  So in production no transaction ever reached `POSTED`, not one `LedgerBalanceSnapshot` row was ever created, and every posted-balance read answered zero permanently. Savings interest accrues on posted balances only (`SPEC-ECON-001` §9.2), so no student could earn interest — the accrual base was structurally pinned at zero. The visible symptom was a student's savings card reading $50.00 directly above a projection captioned "based on current posted balance of $0.00", flat across twelve months. Both reads were individually correct; the ledger beneath them had never settled.

  `ledger_settlement` is now a registered hourly job. Because `savings_interest_payout` is also hourly and two interval jobs have no ordering guarantee between them — and because an unsettled base makes interest *underpay silently* rather than fail — `run_savings_interest_job` discharges the dependency itself by settling first, rather than trusting registration order.

  The reason the suite never caught it is worth recording: `tests/helpers/ledger.py` deliberately exposes `settle_ledger_balances`, so every balance test supplied the trigger production was missing. Every such test was green *because the test settled on its own behalf*. And nothing in `tests/` referenced `init_scheduled_tasks`, `add_job` or `get_jobs` at all, so job registration was wholly unguarded — which is how a correct function nobody scheduled reached a deployment candidate. `init_scheduled_tasks` is skipped under `TESTING`, so registration could not be asserted without starting a real `BackgroundScheduler` in pytest. The ten inline `scheduler.add_job` calls are therefore replaced by a declared `SCHEDULED_JOB_SPECS` tuple that `init_scheduled_tasks` iterates, making membership, trigger and ordering assertable without a scheduler. New `tests/test_ledger_settlement_job.py` (8 passed) drives the job rather than the helper, and its three registration and ordering guards were verified load-bearing by removing the spec and the ordering call, which fails them.

- **The savings page printed two different balances and reconciled neither (2026-09-11)** — The savings card reads posted + pending; the projection caption and the interest forecast read posted alone. Both are correct — `SPEC-ECON-001` §9.2 makes accrual posted-only and §10 requires the projection to use the same rule as execution — but the page quoted both numbers with nothing connecting them. With settlement now running the gap is a short window rather than permanent, and it is still real: between a deposit and the next sweep a student sees a card reading $50.00 above a caption stating their posted balance is $0.00.

  The caption now names the card's figure and says why it differs, and only when it actually differs. Pinned by `tests/test_transfer_savings_projection_route.py::test_unsettled_savings_are_disclosed_rather_than_left_contradicting`, which asserts both directions — the disclosure appears while the deposit is pending and is gone once settlement makes the two figures agree — so the condition is proven to track state rather than being permanently on.

- **Eleven student pages had a help panel that said nothing (2026-09-10)** — The teacher shell got this treatment first; the student shell was left with the contract and none of the content. Eleven of the twelve templates extending `layout_student.html` overrode no `contextual_help_body` and opened to the placeholder sentence.

  The student case was the worse of the two. `layout_admin.html` renders its "Need Help?" trigger only when a doc path resolves, so a teacher page with nothing to say showed no button at all. `layout_student.html` renders the trigger unconditionally and forces a fallback `help_doc_path`, so every one of those pages offered help and then answered with a placeholder — spending a student's trust on a dead end, for the audience least able to route around it.

  Panels are now written for rent, finances, payroll, store, insurance marketplace, policy detail, claim filing, issue reporting, help & support, adding a class, and teacher-recovery verification. The recovery panel is deliberately blunt about social engineering: a student who is asked out of band to confirm a "teacher" recovery is being attacked, and the panel says to stop and tell an adult. `tests/test_docs_help_links.py::test_every_student_page_explains_itself` now fails if a student template is added without one.

  The rent page was found late, and by fixing the guard rather than by reading the templates. `_templates_extending` matched only `extends "layout_student.html"` — the double-quoted form — while `student_rent.html` uses single quotes, which Jinja accepts identically. So the one student page the guard could not see was the one page still shipping the placeholder, and the guard reported the portal clean. It now matches both quote styles, which is a reminder that a green guard whose selector is narrower than its subject is indistinguishable from a satisfied one.

  The claim-filing panel was also split by claim type. It described date and hours fields and a filing deadline as though they were universal; `student_file_claim.html` renders those fields only for productivity policies, and the deadline exists only when the policy declares `claim_window_days`. A transaction claim now gets guidance about choosing the transaction it attaches to, and the deadline topic renders only for a policy that has one — help must describe the form on the screen, not a sibling form.

- **A posted ledger row could be walked backwards through its lifecycle (2026-09-10)** — `_guard_ledger_immutability` enforces `INV-LED-002` over the monetary and identity facts, and deliberately left `status` alone because settlement genuinely rewrites it. But "settlement writes it" is not "anything may write it": `ledger_settlement_service` is the only post-insert writer and only ever moves `PENDING` to `POSTED`, and `INV-LED-003` requires a void or reversal to arrive as a **new linked row** rather than as an edit to the original. `POSTED` → `PENDING`, anything → `VOID`, and a revived `VOID` were all accepted — each one a rewrite of a settled fact wearing a lifecycle costume, and the last two would have changed a balance without leaving a correction behind.

  The guard now rejects every status write except the single lawful forward transition. This was found through its own test: `test_INV_LED_002__status_still_advances_through_settlement` seeded a **posted** row, set it back to `PENDING`, and asserted the reversal succeeded — so the one test named for the lawful transition was passing *because* the unlawful one was permitted, and would have kept passing no matter how the guard drifted. It now seeds `PENDING` and asserts `POSTED`, with a parametrized sibling pinning all four rejected directions.

- **Ledger immutability was enforced only as deep as the ORM (2026-09-10)** — `_guard_ledger_immutability` is a SQLAlchemy `before_update` listener, and a mapper-level listener fires only for ORM unit-of-work flushes. A Core `update()`, a bulk `Query.update()`, `db.session.execute(text(...))`, a migration, a maintenance script, or a `psql` session all bypassed it completely. This was not hypothetical: this branch shipped a fixture in `tests/test_insurance_transaction_economics.py` that backdated a posted row by going around the mapper *on purpose*, with a comment saying so. A guard whose own test suite documents the way around it is a convention, not an invariant.

  `DOM-LED-001 §VII` states the protected fields are immutable "once inserted", without qualifying by access path, so migration `f1a9c3e60b72` moves the enforcement to where the write lands. `ledger_transaction_no_rewrite` mirrors the ORM guard in plpgsql: the monetary and identity facts may not change at all; the write-once columns may go `NULL` → value exactly once; `status` may only advance `PENDING` → `POSTED`. `feat_code` stays free, because `_enforce_transaction_integrity` restamps it with the FEAT performing the lawful update.

  The insurance fixture is fixed rather than exempted. It needed a loss dated eight days ago, and the Ledger posting boundary accepts no caller-supplied time on purpose — when a monetary effect happened is a fact the Ledger stamps. So the fixture now moves the clock the insert reads instead of rewriting the row afterwards, which keeps the fiction in the test's notion of "now" and leaves the row immutable from the moment it exists.

  `INV-ARC-017` notes, both scope limits recorded rather than glossed:

  - This covers `UPDATE` only, and that is the correct and complete scope, now written down as `DOM-LED-001 §VII.2`. The question a `DELETE` trigger raised — whether removing a seat may erase that seat's ledger history — was initially surfaced as an open finding, then resolved at the domain level rather than in a migration. Immutability governs *mutation of a surviving class universe*; cascade deletion arising from lawful seat or class destruction is lifecycle destruction, not an edit to surviving financial history. There is never a lawful state in which the seat is gone but the seat's money remains, so a general `DELETE` prohibition would assert the inverse rule — that ledger history must outlive the entity whose existence gives it meaning — and would abort lawful actor removal.
  - A related concern was raised and then disproved by reachability rather than by assertion: `actor_seat_id` cascades, and a teacher seat can be the actor on rows owned by student seats, which looked like a partial-destruction hazard. It is not reachable. `classes.teacher_user_id → users ON DELETE CASCADE`, so a teacher principal cannot outlive its classes; both live call sites of `delete_seat_with_profile` reject a non-student seat (`remove_pending_student_seat`, FEAT-CLASS-002); and the third reference, `app/routes/admin.py:141`, is a dead import. Teacher-attributed rows are reachable by cascade only when the whole class universe is already being destroyed. `actor_seat_id` / `target_seat_id` are provenance and participation references; `seat_id` carries economic ownership.
