  It was never a cross-tenant leak; `class_id` was on every query, and every read site derived `section` from the same `class_id` it queried with, so the two could not disagree. The failure runs the other way. `uq_payroll_settings_active_scope` is UNIQUE on `class_id` alone `WHERE availability_state = 'IN_USE'`, so a class has **exactly one** selectable policy row. The reader asked for `block = <section>` and on a miss fell back to `block IS NULL`; whenever that single row's `block` was a non-null string not equal to the class's section, **both queries missed the only row that existed** and every seat in the class was paid `DEFAULT_PAY_RATE_PER_SECOND`. No error, no log — just wrong money. Measured against a fixture configured at 2.00/minute, an 8× silent underpayment. Three routes into that state, none exotic: `ClassEconomy.section` is mutable, so configuring payroll and then renaming the section is sufficient; `block` sits in `PayrollSettings._FROZEN_POLICY_FIELDS`, so it is submittable and carried forward from the predecessor on every subsequent submission, propagating a stale value untouched and never revalidating it; and rows predating the class_id-only scope. The writer was already canonical — `upsert_payroll_settings` scopes by `class_id` alone, and the model's own `__table_args__` comment states that `block` "is display metadata and is never a scoping key" — so writer and reader disagreed about what identifies a policy, and only the reader was wrong.

  Two further instances of the same defect class were found by sweeping for it rather than fixing only what was reported. `insurance_claim_feat._resolve_hourly_pay_rate` filtered `block IS NULL`, so a class whose policy row carried *any* label adjudicated PRODUCTIVITY claims at the fallback rate — a claim and a payroll run priced the same work differently. And `scheduled_tasks.py` resolved the daily limit only `if class_row.section`, so a class with no section label never had its configured limit enforced by auto tap-out at all; that one fails permissive, which is why nothing had reported it.

  `_fetch_active_setting(*, class_id)` is now the single canonical reader and `class_id` is the whole scope. `get_pay_rate_for_block(block, *, class_id)` is replaced by `get_pay_rate_for_class(*, class_id)`, and `get_daily_limit_seconds` loses its `block` parameter. Because the partial unique index guarantees one row, a second row means the index is not holding, so the reader **raises** rather than tie-breaking on newest — picking a winner there would let a schema failure present itself as a pay rate. `_get_batch_pay_rates` is keyed by `class_id` with deterministic ordering so the batch reader that `calculate_payroll_breakdown` prices from cannot disagree with the scalar reader a student is shown; that agreement, not either value alone, is what `tests/dom/prod/test_payroll_rate_scope_is_class_id.py` pins. `app/feats/prod.py` loses a duplicated hardcoded fallback and delegates to the shared reader, keeping its class-existence guard while dropping the dead label binding. The regression file is 6 tests, verified non-vacuous by replicating the pre-fix query verbatim against the same fixture — old reader `0.004166…`, new reader `0.033333…` — because a test that passes for the wrong reason is this same failure class one level up.

- **The audit signing key was enforced everywhere except where the invariant says it must be (2026-09-06)** — INV-ARC-016 §VII requires `AUDIT_HMAC_KEY` to be "required at application startup. The application shall refuse to start if this variable is absent." It was not in `required_env_vars` in `app/__init__.py`, so it wasn't checked at startup at all. `audit_service` does raise on a missing key, but it wraps its own `_load_signing_key()` in `try/except RuntimeError` so the module can import under pytest before fixtures run, leaving `_SIGNING_KEY = b""`. The net behavior — confirmed by booting the app with the key emptied, not by reading the code — was that a deployment missing the key started clean, passed every health check, and then raised `AuditContextError` at the *first audited write*: mid-transaction, inside a FEAT, in front of a user. The system failed closed, which is the correct direction, but at the worst possible moment and on a box that had looked healthy since deploy. Deferred enforcement is not enforcement; "refuse to start" can only be honored at start. `AUDIT_HMAC_KEY` is now in `required_env_vars`.

  Two pieces of documentation were actively misleading here and are corrected. `audit_protected()` in `app/feats/base.py` documented itself as doing "nothing if `AUDIT_HMAC_KEY` is absent (dev/test without the key configured — CI enforces the key in production)". Every clause was false: it re-raises `AuditContextError` rather than doing nothing, no CI job references `AUDIT_HMAC_KEY` anywhere (`grep` across `.github/` and `.ci/` returns nothing), and a fail-open audit hook would itself violate INV-ARC-016. That docstring was the more dangerous half of the defect — someone auditing for a fail-open gap would have found one *described* there and stopped, while the actual gap sat upstream in the startup gate. Separately, the README's `.env` setup block omitted the key entirely, so following the quickstart now produced a hard boot failure; it also used a **quoted** `<< 'EOF'` heredoc, which suppresses `$(...)` expansion and wrote the literal text `$(python3 -c "import secrets; ...")` into `.env` as the value of every secret. Both are fixed. `tests/test_startup_env_requirements.py` parametrizes over the whole required list — so removing *any* name from the gate fails a test — and includes a negative control, because a gate that rejected every environment would otherwise pass.

- **The session cookie outlived every session it represented (2026-09-06)** — three authoritative session lifetimes already existed in `app/auth.py`: a student's hard cap from login (`User.current_session_expires_at`), a teacher's `SESSION_TIMEOUT_MINUTES` idle limit, and a sysadmin's `SYSTEM_ADMIN_SESSION_TIMEOUT_MINUTES` idle limit. All three are enforced server-side, which means they only fire when a request arrives. Nothing bounded the *cookie*: `PERMANENT_SESSION_LIFETIME` is unset, so it was Flask's 31-day default, and a student who closed the tab left a cookie on the device for a month — carrying the `display_metadata` cache, which holds decrypted first and last names for the student and their teacher. `app/session_lifetime.py` adds `RoleScopedSessionInterface`, which derives the cookie's expiry per role in `get_expiration_time`; the student login flow stamps the authoritative instant into the session so the cookie and the `users` row die together rather than approximately together. No new lifetime is introduced and no existing one is changed — the cookie is made to mirror rules that were already binding. A single global `PERMANENT_SESSION_LIFETIME` could not have expressed this: setting it to the ten-minute student cap would have silently cut sysadmin sessions from 60 minutes to 10, which `test_sysadmin_is_not_truncated_to_the_student_limit` now pins against.

  **`PERMANENT_SESSION_LIFETIME` was deliberately left alone, and this is the record of why.** A session with no recognized role falls through to the Flask default on purpose. A pre-login session holds a CSRF token and flash messages; the two session keys that carry personal data, `display_metadata` and `teacher_display_name_cache`, are both written only after a canonical authenticated context exists. The generic permanent-session default is therefore *not* a PII-retention control, and shortening it would trade a working login form — whose CSRF token would start outliving its own cookie — for protection of data that window never holds. This is written down so that rediscovering the 31-day default six months from now does not lead to "hardening" it for a reason that was already investigated and rejected. `test_permanent_session_lifetime_is_left_at_the_flask_default` pins the non-change; `app/session_lifetime.py`'s module docstring carries the same scope note next to the code.
- **The constitutional evidence runner had never executed a single test (2026-09-05)** — `_command_for` built `[pytest_executable, "-q", path]`, and the workflow passes `--pytest python`, so every piece of evidence ran as `python -q tests/dom/.../test_foo.py` — a bare script invocation, which collects nothing. The gate designed to prove invariants held was running Python files as programs and reading their exit codes. It happened to fail closed rather than pass falsely: all ten evidence modules raise on import when run outside pytest, so each returned `1` and each family reported `FAIL`. That is the safe direction, but it means Constitutional CI would have gone red on every PR for a reason having nothing to do with the change under review, and the natural response to a gate that is always red is to stop believing it. Now `[pytest_executable, "-m", "pytest", "-q", path]`. Verified end to end: `CI-TEMPORAL` reports `26 passed`, where it previously returned exit 1 with an import traceback.
- **Two invariant-family path rules selected on directories that have never existed (2026-09-05)** — `CI-XDOMAIN` listed `app/domains/**` and `app/jobs/**`. `git log` confirms neither path has appeared in this repository's history. Because selection is a union across rules, a dead rule does not error — it simply contributes nothing, and the family quietly narrows. Found by the new manifest self-check on its first run, which is the point of building it: this is the fifth occurrence of the matches-nothing class on this branch, and the first one caught by a machine instead of by reading.
- **The migration idempotency gate rejected the guard-clause form, blocking every migration PR (2026-09-05)** — `check_idempotency` in `scripts/validate-migrations.py` recognized a protected operation only when it was lexically nested inside an `if`. Two committed migrations (`a1c4e7d92f30`, `e6f7a8b9c0d1`) use the equivalent early-return form — `if column_exists(...): return` followed by unindented `op.add_column(...)` — and were reported as unguarded. Neither is a real defect; the protection is identical. But the false positive made the validator exit 1, and the validator is `CI-PERSIST`'s auxiliary evidence, so every migration-touching PR would have failed the constitutional gate for a cause no author could act on. A guard clause now counts as a guard: after an `if` whose body ends in `return` or `raise`, the following siblings in that body are conditional. `tests/test_migration_validator.py` pins the check in both directions — a genuinely unguarded `add_column` still errors, an `if` that falls through still protects nothing, and a guard clause does not leak into a later function — because a validator that has been loosened without a test for the loosening is the next silent gate.
- **`CI-VALIDATION` governed the CI machinery but declared no evidence (2026-09-05)** — the family owns `.github/workflows/**`, `scripts/**`, and `tests/conftest.py` under INV-ARC-017, and its `pass_contract` reads "classifier and evidence metadata validate successfully" — but `evidence_commands` was empty, so it could only ever report `NOT_EVALUATED`. The evidence existed the whole time; it was simply never named. `tests/test_ci_classifier.py`, `tests/test_ci_evidence_runner.py`, and `tests/test_migration_validator.py` are now declared, and a CI-only change reports a real PASS.
- **The classifier ate the leading dot of every dotfile path (2026-09-05)** — `changed_paths`, `select_families`, and `classify` each normalized with `path.strip().lstrip("./")`. `lstrip` removes a character *set*, not a prefix, so `.github/workflows/x` arrived as `github/workflows/x` and `.ci/invariant_families.yml` as `ci/invariant_families.yml`. Neither matched any rule. The consequence was disguised rather than absent: with nothing matched, the fail-closed unknown-path fallback fired and selected `CI-ARC-EXEC`, `CI-SCOPE`, and `CI-VALIDATION` — a set that happens to contain the family a workflow change *should* have selected, so the output looked right while the mechanism was broken, and every CI-configuration change silently pulled in seven unrelated database-backed test modules. `CI-VALIDATION` could never be selected by its own `.github/workflows/**` rule. Now normalized with `removeprefix("./")` through a single shared helper, so the three call sites cannot drift apart. Separately, `.ci/**` was governed by no family at all: the manifest that defines every gate was outside the reach of all of them, and is now a `CI-VALIDATION` path rule. Both are pinned by tests that assert selection happens by rule rather than by fallback, since the fallback is precisely what made this invisible.
- **`CI-RENDER` reported PASS while auditing zero templates (2026-09-05)** — `tests/test_accessibility.py` parametrizes over `ACCESSIBILITY_TEMPLATE_PATHS`, and `constitutional-ci.yml` never set it. An unset variable yields an empty parameter set; pytest reports `got empty parameter set for (template_path)`, exits 0, and the family reports PASS. This is the worst form of the matches-nothing class seen on this branch — the other five either failed closed or reported `NOT_EVALUATED`, and the manifest self-check cannot catch this one because the path rule matches, the file exists, and the command succeeds. The workflow now collects changed templates from the same diff the classifier uses, mirroring `accessibility-gate.yml`, with deletions excluded because the audit renders every file it is given. The gate is still not complete and `known_limits` now says how: the audit skips layouts, macros, components, named fragments, and any template containing `{% extends`, which is 52 of 86 top-level templates, so a PR touching only those still passes on nothing.
- **`CI-PII` declared no evidence, and the PII gate it should have declared had never run, was wrong, and could have passed on nothing (2026-09-05)** — Four separate defects stacked in one family. (1) `CI-PII` governs INV-ARC-005/018/019 and selects on `app/models.py`, `app/services/identity/**`, `migrations/**`, and `tests/dom/identity/**` — nearly every schema or identity change — but `evidence_commands` and `auxiliary_evidence` were both empty, so it could only report `NOT_EVALUATED`, and the fail-closed aggregate meant those PRs failed the constitutional gate for a reason unrelated to the change. (2) `scripts/validate-pii-storage.py` already existed and was already registered in `ALLOWED_AUXILIARY` as `pii_storage_validator`, but **no family referenced it**, so it had never executed once. The manifest self-check cannot catch this: it validates that declared ids resolve, not that registered scripts are declared. (3) It would have failed on the live tree if it had run, because it classified `IdentityProfile.notes` as an unamended §VI identity PII field. That reading was wrong. `notes` is teacher-authored free text about a student, and the application neither inspects nor interprets its contents — it is not one of the identity fields §VI enumerates, and the enforceable contract is its storage *form*: arbitrary author text must be encrypted at rest under §V.2 regardless of what is in it. It is now declared in a separate `OPAQUE_ENCRYPTED` category on exactly that basis, while a *new, undeclared* free-text column on a governed identity class is still a finding under §VII.2, which names those columns as leak vessels. (4) The validator selected its classes and columns by name, so renaming `IdentityProfile` — or dropping an allowlisted column — would have left it auditing nothing and printing success, the same matches-nothing class that produced `CI-RENDER`'s vacuous PASS. Absence is now a finding in both directions: a governed class missing from `app/models.py` fails, and an allowlist entry matching no column fails. Beyond the storage half, **§VIII retention had no evidence anywhere in the tree** — nothing asserted that PII is destroyed with the record that owns it, and a correctly encrypted name that survives the deletion of its seat is still retained PII. `tests/dom/identity/test_pii_retention_deletion.py` exercises all four §VIII rules against a real database, including one test that deletes a seat in raw SQL so the schema's `ON DELETE CASCADE` is what is under test rather than the deletion helper — otherwise any other path that deletes a seat would strand its profile. Every test first asserts the PII it is about to delete actually existed, so a fixture that stopped creating profiles cannot turn them into tests that delete nothing and assert nothing is absent. Verified load-bearing by mutation: weakening the FK to `ON DELETE SET NULL` and removing the ORM cascade fails four of the six. `known_limits` now states plainly what is still not covered, rather than letting the pass imply it: **INV-ARC-005 is not evidenced at all** — nothing gates PII in logs, telemetry, error messages, or URLs, and `DisplayMetadata` serializes decrypted names and the teacher note into the Flask session with no test on that boundary — the storage check is an AST scan that reads neither the live schema nor `migrations/**` and cannot prove a `*_hash` column is HMAC-produced rather than merely named so, and §VII.4 backup retention is outside CI entirely. `tests/test_pii_storage_validator.py` pins the validator in three directions (valid forms not flagged, real defects caught, and cannot pass by auditing nothing) and is declared under `CI-VALIDATION`. `CI-XDOMAIN` is now the only family with no evidence.
- **An attendance test failed for two hours out of every twenty-four (2026-09-05)** — `test_DOM_IDEN_001__enforce_daily_limits_taps_out_when_limit_reached_in_scope` seeded a session at `datetime.now(utc) - 2h` and asserted the tap-out landed at `started_at + limit`. `enforce_daily_limits_job` accrues against a *daily* limit and therefore clamps every interval with `max(active_start, day_start_utc)` — time spent yesterday does not count toward today's cap, which is correct. So whenever the suite runs within two hours of the class's evaluation-day boundary, the seed lands in *yesterday*, the job correctly taps out at `day_start + limit`, and the test fails for a reason unrelated to what it certifies. Production is right; the test was wall-clock fragile. All three seeds in the file now derive from the same `evaluation_day_boundaries` primitive the job uses, so the scenario they describe ("a session opened earlier today") is constructible at every hour rather than only at twenty-two of them. The assertion loses no strength: away from the boundary the seed is unchanged, and at the boundary it still verifies the limit arithmetic. Found because the identity suite happened to run just after local midnight — the same run passed in full ninety minutes earlier.
- **A support ticket from one student could reverse another student's transaction (2026-09-05)** — Regression introduced by this branch's own B7 fix and caught by the full suite, which is the reason it is written up rather than quietly squashed. B7 replaced `admin.resolve_issue`'s seat-ownership guard with a class-tenancy guard, on the reasoning that "what must be true before a teacher mutates a ledger row is that the row belongs to the class this teacher is acting in." That is true, and it was genuinely missing — but it is an *additional* condition, not a substitute. Two independent things must hold: **tenancy** (the row belongs to the acting class) and **binding** (the row belongs to the seat that submitted *this* issue). Dropping the second left same-class, different-seat reversals wide open: any student's ticket became a lever for reversing any classmate's transaction, and because DOM-SUP-001 defines `issue_resolution_actions` as a declaration log whose rows are append-only, the log would have permanently attributed that reversal to the wrong actor — a defect that survives the ledger correction. Both conditions are now asserted, and the seat lookup carries the `class_id` predicate B7 was right to demand (DOM-IDEN-001 §VI: `Seat.public_id` is only answerable with a class in hand). The same guard was applied to the sibling `compensating_transaction` branch, which moves money by the same mechanism and had the same gap. `tests/dom/interpretation/test_issue_resolution_reverse_transaction.py` — which certifies precisely the same-class/different-seat case, and which the B7 commit did not touch — was the only red test in a 1208-test run and is the reason this was found before merge.
- **Three CI workflows gated on a branch that does not exist, and the migration gate had never once run (2026-09-05)** — `actionlint.yml`, `check-migrations.yml`, and `policy-guardrails.yml` all filtered on `codex/v2.0`. That ref is absent both locally and on the remote (`git branch -a | grep -c codex/v2.0` returns `0`); the integration branch is `CTH_v2.0`. The consequences were not uniform, and the difference is the point. `actionlint` and `policy-guardrails` declare their `pull_request` leg without a branch filter, so both still ran on PRs and only their push legs were dead — but `policy-guardrails` additionally guards its `guardrails-main` job on `github.ref == 'refs/heads/codex/v2.0'`, a condition that can never be true, so the **strict `--no-waivers` check had never executed a single time**: waivers were accepted on every PR and never re-checked without them. `check-migrations` filtered *both* legs and was therefore wholly inert — no migration has ever been validated by CI, in a repository whose own documentation names migrations as the leading cause of deploy failure. All three now name `CTH_v2.0`, and `check-migrations` matches any target branch on the PR leg so that a gate of that consequence cannot go silently dead again the next time a branch is renamed. A second, independent defect in the same file: its `paths` filter watched `app/models/**`, but this repo has `app/models.py` — a module, not a package — so the filter would not have matched model changes even with the branch name corrected. Both spellings are now listed, matching the convention `schema-gate.yml` already used. Not fixed here, because it is a release decision rather than a defect: `deploy.yml` deploys production on push to `main`, and `main` is **1053 commits behind** `CTH_v2.0`, so promoting v2 requires an explicit choice between merging to `main` and repointing the trigger. Recorded in the tracker's new §V launch checklist.

- **Track T2 — the sysadmin escalation surface: a hard 500, a consent gap, a missing FEAT envelope, and unscoped seat resolution (2026-09-05)** — Four blockers, all found by source review rather than by a failing test, and all now pinned by `tests/dom/support/test_escalation_disclosure_and_scope.py`.
  - **B4 (500 on every sysadmin escalation view).** `_issue_to_view` in `app/routes/system_admin.py` read `issue.teacher.get_sysadmin_display_name()` — a relationship and a method that exist nowhere on `Issue`. The deeper cause was that `Issue.reviewer_public_id`, the column that exists precisely to record the reviewing teacher, **had no writer anywhere in the codebase**, so the view had no lawful source for the reviewer and reached for a fabricated one. The fix adds the writer: `admin.escalate_issue` now stamps `reviewer_public_id` with the teacher's class-scoped seat `public_id` (DOM-SUP-001 §VII, INV-ARC-019 §IX — sysadmins never see raw `seat_id`/`user_id`), and the three sysadmin templates render that instead of a name, falling back to `Unattributed`.
  - **B5 (class-name disclosure).** `issues.class_label` is specified by DOM-SUP-001 §VI as a class context cache "frozen at submission time" that "must not be re-fetched live from ClassEconomy after submission." The column had never been created. It is added by migration `a1c4e7d92f30` and written at both submission sites (`app/utils/issue_helpers.create_issue`, `app/services/issue_service.create_support_ticket`). Resolving it live from `class_public_id` would have avoided the migration but broken the freeze: a class renamed — or destroyed — after submission would silently rewrite or erase the context of tickets already in flight. Backfill is an explicit one-time reconstruction from the live class row; unmatched rows stay `NULL`. Disclosure is now gated on `share_class_name_with_sysadmin` (default false) **at the view-dict boundary** rather than only in markup — a consent-gated value must not travel to the view layer and rely on a template to hide it.
  - **B6 (missing mutation envelope).** `admin.escalate_issue` mutated issue state with no FEAT boundary. It now carries `@requires_feat_context("FEAT-SUP-001")`.
  - **B7 (unscoped seat resolution).** Six sites resolved a `Seat` from a `public_id` with no `class_id` filter, or fell back to a guess when class scope was absent. Under DOM-IDEN-001 §VI a `User` holds one `Seat` per `Class`, so "the seat for this public_id" is only answerable with a class in hand. `app/access/scope_factory.resolve_scope` no longer falls back to `claimed_seats[0]` — the lowest-id seat across every class the user participates in, written into the request as though it had been chosen, which is the shape of the P0 same-teacher multi-period leak — and instead raises `AccessScopeDenied`. `admin._resolve_student_detail_seat`, `admin._resolve_issue_identity`, and the `issues_queue` bulk actor-name lookup are all scoped by the active class and degrade to the truncated `public_id` rather than resolving names cross-tenant. `admin.resolve_issue` adds a `class_id` predicate to its `submitter_seat` lookup and adds the tenancy question its guard had never asked — `transaction.class_id == class_id` — alongside the issue-binding question it already asked. `system_admin.resolve_escalated_issue` resolves the reward class *first* and scopes the seat to it, refusing the reward if either is unresolvable; previously it could credit a seat in one class while writing the ledger row with `class_id=None`. The dead, defective `_resolve_seat` helper in `app/services/identity_service.py` is deleted rather than repaired: it had no callers, and its signature poses a question the canonical model cannot answer.
- **The last 14 suite failures were identity *test* defects, not identity *domain* defects (2026-09-05)** — After T5 closed, the 16 remaining full-suite failures were two classes; the 14-failure class in `tests/dom/identity/test_admin_membership_gates.py` and `test_multi_teacher_hardening.py` had been provisionally attributed to track T2 (blockers B4–B7). Triage found otherwise: 13 of the 14 were a single harness defect, `FEATContextError: Nested FEAT context forbidden — Active=FEAT-IDEN-001, attempted=FEAT-IDEN-001`, raised from `initialize()`. The initializer opens its **own** `FEAT-IDEN-001` to provision a classroom, so wrapping a call to it inside a test-owned `FEATContext` composes a FEAT with itself, which INV-ARC-000 §VIII.2 forbids — the same defect already corrected in `test_banking_core.py`. Provisioning is hoisted out of the test's context at every site; where the test genuinely needs an envelope for its *own* mutations, the envelope is retained and now opens after provisioning, with the reason stated in-line. Both files already contained correct examples, so the fix matches their own established convention. The 14th failure was a real test defect of a different kind and is corrected rather than deleted: `test_DOM_IDEN_007__remove_student_from_teacher_scope_preserves_shared_student` asserted a seat's `IdentityProfile` **survived** deletion of its seat, which contradicts DOM-IDEN-001 §VI — a profile is display data *for a Seat within a Class*, 1:1 and cascade-deleted with it — and the test never actually shared a student, provisioning a second classroom it then never used, so the scenario in its name was never constructed. It now builds the sharing it claims (a second `Seat` for the same `User` in class B with its own class-local profile), removes the class A seat, and asserts what the canonical model actually requires: the class A seat *and its profile* are gone, while the `User`, the class B seat, and the class B profile survive — the participation law, which is the property the test was named for. **Both files now pass in full (22 passed, from 14 failed / 8 passed).** This clears noise that was *masking* signal: no genuine B4–B7 failure could be assessed until it was gone, and none is present — B4–B7 remain open on source review, not on a red test.

- **The public-page accessibility checks describe the two-host arrangement they actually guard (2026-09-05)** — Two tests had become permanently red, both as fallout from making the marketing pages self-sufficient for publication. `test_public_pages_use_local_icon_font_contract` asserted the CDN font was *absent* from `github-pages/*.html`, which encoded a single-host assumption: the published artifact contains only `github-pages/`, so a relative `../static/` path cannot resolve there and the source must name a CDN, while `github_pages_asset` rewrites that link back to the same-origin font for the app-served copy that CSP actually governs. The test now owns the published half only — a page that uses icons must load an icon font, and no page may reference a path outside the artifact — with the app-served half left to `tests/dom/platform/test_security_headers.py`, which already pinned it. The requirement keys on actual icon use rather than a filename allow-list, so `index.html` is exempt because it has no icons rather than because of its name. Separately, `test_axe_compliance.py` drives a real browser against a running dev server and already skipped when Playwright or Chromium was missing, but raised `ERR_CONNECTION_REFUSED` when no server was listening; since it is not part of the accessibility CI gate, that made it a permanent failure in every ordinary suite run. All three prerequisites now skip alike, so a failure means a page genuinely has a violation. Confirmed the skip retires nothing: with a server up the audit runs and passes on all seven public routes against wcag2a/wcag2aa.

- **Posted-balance snapshots are read and written per account, not per seat (2026-09-05)** — `LedgerBalanceSnapshot` still carried the pre-split `posted_checking_balance_cents` / `posted_savings_balance_cents` pair, while the migration chain had already moved the table to the canonical `(class_id, seat_id, account_type)` identity of DOM-LED-001 §2. Every read went through a snapshot lookup that named no account, so whichever row happened to come back was reported as *both* balances: a seat holding savings could read a non-zero checking balance it did not have, and settlement wrote one account's total over another's. The model now matches the schema; `ledger_service` and `balance_service` scope reads by `account_type`; and `settle_balances` locks each applicable account row in deterministic account order against a single settlement boundary, per INV-LED-009. A missing snapshot row stays a normal state that recomputes from ledger history rather than an error (INV-LED-006). Regression coverage in `tests/dom/ledger/test_balance_snapshot_account_scope.py`, which fails against the pre-fix commit with `UndefinedColumn: column ledger_balance_snapshot.posted_checking_balance_cents does not exist`. Three fixes came along with it, because they were the same defect seen from other angles: the historical `f2c9d1a6b7e8` shape guard now tolerates both snapshot shapes (a bootstrapped database materializes today's ORM schema at step 0, so a present-day model correction had retroactively broken a historical step); four banking tests no longer nest `initialize()` inside their own FEAT context (INV-ARC-000 §VIII.2); and the idempotent-transaction-type pin now includes `rent_payment`, which the canonical rent obligation model added without updating the enumeration. The three ledger ops scripts that still imported the removed `BalanceCache` were swept to the canonical name in the same pass.

- **The store item edit form renders again — `edit_store_item` had lost its template name (2026-09-05)** — `GET /admin/store/edit/<item_id>` raised `TypeError: render_template_with_fallback() missing 1 required positional argument: 'template_name'` and returned 500, so a teacher could not open the edit form for any store item. The call passed only keyword arguments; the `'admin_edit_item.html'` positional was deleted in `bad800fa` ("Complete FEAT context boundary reconstruction") as collateral damage from one of the 34 `db.session.commit()` removals in that commit, which is why it reads as an unrelated refactor. `git log -L` attributes the line to `4f2e7dc3`, but that commit's diff explicitly *re-added* the template name while reformatting the call to multi-line — it only rewrote the surrounding range. The POST path redirects on success and never renders, which is how a wholly dead GET survived: `admin_edit_item.html` sat orphaned, with no route in the codebase rendering it. Restored the argument; confirmed the template is the right one rather than assumed, since it references only `form`, `item`, and `expected_weekly_hours` (the last already guarded with `is defined`), posts to `url_for('admin.edit_store_item', item_id=item.id)`, and every one of the 23 `form.*` fields it renders exists on `StoreItemForm`. An AST-shaped sweep of `app/` for the same defect (a `render_template(` whose first argument is a keyword) found no other instance. New `tests/test_admin_store_edit_route.py` (2 passed) pins a teacher GET at 200 with the item rendered, and a foreign-class item at 404 so the class-scope guard on this route stays covered; the first fails against the pre-fix tree with the exact `TypeError`, verified by stash cycle.

- **The landing page's sign-in buttons reach the application from both hosts (2026-09-04)** — After launch the marketing site is published from `github-pages/` to `classroomtokenhub.com` while the application runs on `app.classroomtokenhub.com`, so `landing.html`'s three root-relative sign-in links (`/admin/login`, `/student/login`, `/sysadmin/login`) would have resolved against GitHub Pages and 404'd — nobody could sign in from the front door. The published markup now points at the absolute application origin. That alone would have broken the opposite case: the *same file* is also served by the application itself at `/gh/landing.html`, so that a certification run stays entirely on the local origin, and absolute URLs would have sent a tester's sign-in click to **production**. `github_pages_asset` therefore rewrites HTML on the way out, mapping the production application origin back to the serving origin. The same hook restores the same-origin font stylesheet for that app-served copy only: the published pages must load fonts from a CDN because the Pages artifact contains just `github-pages/` and cannot reach `static/`, but when the application serves that markup it is subject to the application's own CSP (GitHub Pages sends no such header), which blocks the CDN and would drop every Material Symbols glyph, re-exposing ligature names as visible text — the defect fixed on 2026-09-03. One source file, correct under both hosts' constraints. Regression coverage in `tests/dom/platform/test_security_headers.py` pins both rewrites.

- **Landing sign-up note icon/text alignment (2026-09-04)** — Adjusted the landing-page sign-up note title layout so the `person_add` symbol and question text align on the same visual line instead of the icon sitting higher, with a subtle optical vertical nudge for more consistent rendering across browsers.

- **A rent change no longer rewrites the past — `rent_settings` is an append-only policy repository (2026-09-04)** — Closes blocker **B1**. `ObligationAssessment` has no amount column: the amount a student owes for a cycle is resolved at read time from the `rent_settings` row addressed by the `policy_uuid` the assessment froze when it was created. That freeze was already wired end to end — `reconcile_rent` stamps `settings.policy_uuid` onto every new `BillCycle` and every new `ObligationAssessment` — but it was **inert**, because `rent_settings` was a mutable singleton: `UNIQUE(class_id)`, one row per class, updated in place by `setattr`. The `policy_uuid` an assessment froze therefore kept pointing at a row whose terms changed underneath it. A teacher raising rent from 50 to 200 in October retroactively repriced every already-assessed, already-*paid* September obligation to 200, silently converting settled students into debtors — and the same row is what late-fee amounts, grace periods, and rent-perk grants resolve through, so the rewrite reached the penalties and entitlements too. Per DOM-POL-001 §VI.0/§VI.1 the `policy_uuid` **is** the version and any submission — first-time or resubmission — is a *new contract*, not an edit: "the backend MUST NOT infer whether a change is meaningful." `RentSettings` is now that repository. The `UNIQUE(class_id)` index is dropped (many versions per class is the normal case), an `availability_state` column (`IN_USE`/`HIDDEN`/`RETIRED`, CHECK-constrained) provides the **mutable projection over the immutable row** — a projection, never a version pointer — and a `before_update` guard rejects in-place writes to any definition-payload column outright, so the repricing defect cannot be reintroduced by a stray `setattr`. `get_rent_settings()` is now a deterministic current-policy reader (newest `IN_USE` by `rent_configured_at`, `id`), and `admin_settings_service` exposes `create_rent_settings` / `supersede_rent_settings`, the latter carrying unspecified fields forward from the predecessor so a partial submission still yields a complete contract. Both write paths — the `/admin/rent-settings` POST and `economy_rebalance` — mint new rows; `rent_payment_feat` resolves perks through **the assessment's** `policy_uuid` rather than the live row. Migration `d5e6f7a8b9c0` is fully guarded (its `downgrade` is lossy by construction and documented as such: collapsing to a singleton means discarding exactly the superseded versions historical assessments resolve through, so it retains the newest `IN_USE` row per class). Along the way, **`/admin/rent-settings` turned out to be dead in production** for a B9-class reason: the route was decorated `@requires_feat_context("FEAT-OBL-003")` — Obligations / "Scheduled Insurance Cycle", the wrong domain — while its handler opens `FEAT-SETTINGS-001`, so every POST raised `FEATContextError: Nested FEAT context forbidden` and a teacher could not change rent *at all*. The decorator is removed rather than re-pointed, because `requires_feat_context` reads `idempotency_key` from `kwargs` — which a Flask view never receives — and would have discarded the route's payload-derived key; the handler's own `FEAT-SETTINGS-001` is the single envelope, which is also what MAP-UI-001 assigns (rent policy configuration is Class Configuration authority, not Obligations mutation). An AST sweep of every FEAT-decorated route found four more instances of the identical defect, all likewise dead: `/admin/rent-waiver/add` and `/student/rent/pay/<period>` decorated with the same FEAT their delegate already carries, and `/admin/store`, `/admin/store/edit/<id>`, `/admin/item/deactivate/<id>` decorated `FEAT-STOR-001` over an inner `FEAT-SETTINGS-001` — a store *catalog* edit is configuration, not a purchase. All five now open exactly one envelope, at the layer that owns it. New `tests/dom/obligations/test_rent_policy_immutability.py` (8 passed) pins both halves: supersession mints a new row and retires its predecessor, unspecified terms carry forward, an in-place payload edit raises, availability may still change on an existing row (and a `HIDDEN` policy disappears from the current-policy read); and — the regression proper, verified to fail against the pre-fix commit with `assert Decimal('200.00') == Decimal('50.00')` — a rent increase does not reprice an existing assessment, every seat's assessment holds its own amount, the late-fee amount is frozen too, and `reconcile_rent` re-runs do not adopt a new policy for an already-open cycle.

- **A pay raise no longer reprices work already done — `payroll_settings` and `hall_pass_settings` are append-only policy repositories (2026-09-05)** — Closes blocker **B2** and, with it, fix track T1. `PayrollSettings` was the mutable singleton DOM-CLASS-003 §XI.4 names as prohibited: `upsert_payroll_settings` fetched the class's one row and reassigned fields on it via `setattr`, and the table had **no `policy_uuid` column at all**, so nothing downstream could address a specific version. That made `PayrollEvent`'s policy freeze decorative. A payroll run pays out *all* attendance accrued since the seat's last payroll event, and `_resolve_pay_rate_per_second` prices it from the class's current policy row — so rewriting that row rewrote the terms under which already-worked time was priced, and left no surviving version that remembered the old rate. DOM-CLASS-003 ("Pending Next-Cycle Payroll-Governing Changes") is explicit that a payroll-governing change MUST NOT mutate the policy governing the open cycle (INV-ARC-015 §VI.7). Both tables now follow the append-only pattern B1 established: a `policy_uuid` that **is** the version, a CHECK-constrained `availability_state` (`IN_USE`/`HIDDEN`/`RETIRED`) supplying the mutable projection *over* the immutable row, and a `before_update` guard that rejects in-place writes to any definition-payload column so the defect cannot return through a stray `setattr`. A partial unique index on `class_id WHERE availability_state = 'IN_USE'` does double duty: it forces supersession to retire the predecessor in the same transaction that inserts its replacement, and it closes the TOCTOU race where two concurrent submissions each observe no current policy and both insert. `block` is deliberately **not** part of that index — it is display metadata and never a scoping key (INV-ARC-019), and including it would permit two concurrently-current policies for one class that the `class_id`-scoped reader could not disambiguate. `upsert_payroll_settings` keeps its name so the many call sites stay stable but no longer updates anything: it retires the predecessor, carries forward every field the submission did not mention (a partial form post still yields a complete contract), rejects unknown fields rather than silently dropping them, and inserts a new row — then activates the payroll `PolicyVersion` snapshot as before. `is_active` is **replaced** by `availability_state` rather than kept beside it, since two projections would be precisely the alternative current-version pointer DOM-POL-001 §VI.0 prohibits; all seven read sites across `admin.py`, `scheduled_tasks.py`, `payroll.py`, `prod.py`, and `insurance_claim_feat.py` were converted, while `PolicyVersion.is_active` — a different lineage — was left untouched. `next_payroll_date` stays mutable by design: it is the recurring schedule's cursor, advanced by `run_automatic_payroll_job` after each completed run, not part of the definition a teacher submits. `HallPassSettings` already inserted a new row per save, so the append-only half looked done; what was missing was retiring the predecessor, without which a class accumulated several rows all claiming to be current and the reader picked one by sort order. Its read path carried a second defect — `_get_or_create_hall_pass_settings` inserted a default policy as a side effect of being *asked* which policy applied. That is **not** an INV-ARC-007 GET-write (its only live caller was the queue-settings write command), but it did mint a governing contract nobody submitted and leave that conjured row unretired alongside the submission that followed it. It is now a pure reader and callers with no policy fall back to class defaults; the dead route shim in `app/routes/api.py` is deleted. A **third** defect surfaced while editing that function and is fixed here rather than left to ship: `update_hall_pass_queue_settings` carried `@requires_feat_context("FEAT-SETTINGS-001")` and then called `save_hall_pass_setup_config`, which carries it too. That decorator opens a context unconditionally, so a FEAT composed a FEAT and **every** call raised `FEATContextError` — making the queue-limit API endpoint (`app/routes/api.py`) an unconditional 500 for as long as the nesting had been in place. This is the same shape as B9 and the five routes B1 uncovered; the decorator is removed from the outer function, which is a thin argument-preparer over the inner command that owns the envelope (INV-ARC-000 §VIII.2, INV-ARC-021 §V.2). It was found by the B2 suite, not by the endpoint's own coverage, which is why the pin lives in `tests/dom/attendance/test_hall_pass_policy.py`. `policy_versions` rows with `domain='payroll'` were left alone: they are DOM-CLASS-003 economic-policy evolution, a separate lineage with its own boundary-activation rules, not this table's version pointer, so `PayrollEvent.policy_version_id` stays. Migration `3bb29ef4e874` is fully guarded and tested up → down → up; it backfills `availability_state` from `is_active`, retires all but the newest current row per class *before* creating the unique index, and then drops `is_active`. New `tests/dom/prod/test_payroll_policy_immutability.py` (9 passed) and expanded `tests/dom/attendance/test_hall_pass_policy.py` (6 passed) pin both halves: submission mints a new row and retires its predecessor with exactly one `IN_USE` row the reader agrees with, superseded policies stay readable by uuid, unspecified terms carry forward, unknown fields and in-place payload edits raise, availability may still change on an existing row, the schedule cursor stays writable, the queue-settings command executes as a single FEAT — and the regression proper, which fails against the pre-fix commit where a raise from 0.25 to 2.00 leaves exactly one `payroll_settings` row saying 2.00 with no version that remembers 0.25. Verified by stash cycle: **9 of the 14 new tests fail against the pre-fix tree and all 14 pass after**. One of the five that passed pre-fix — `test_reading_hall_pass_settings_never_mints_a_policy` — is honestly relabeled in-file as a *guard* rather than a regression pin, because it exercises the query-service reader that was already pure; the impure `_get_or_create` sat behind a different entry point. Two existing tests (`tests/dom/class/test_payroll_settings_class_scope.py`, `tests/dom/identity/test_admin_membership_gates.py`) used `.one()` on `payroll_settings`, which append-only legitimately breaks; both now read through `get_payroll_settings` rather than loosening to `.first()`, which would have reinstated exactly the sort-order guessing this change removes.

- **Daily-limit auto tap-out actually runs again — the enforcement job was FEAT-PROD-001 calling FEAT-PROD-001 (2026-09-04)** — Closes blocker **B9**. `enforce_daily_limits_job` (`app/scheduled_tasks.py`) is decorated `@requires_feat_context("FEAT-PROD-001")` and, for each seat past the daily cap, called `record_attendance_session`, which carried the *same* decorator. Exactly one FEAT executes per invocation (INV-ARC-000 §VIII.2, INV-ARC-021 §V.2), so the inner envelope raised `FEATContextError: Nested FEAT context forbidden` — and a per-seat `except Exception: logger.error(...); continue` **swallowed it**. The job therefore logged a tidy per-seat error and reported success while closing **zero** sessions: every student past their daily limit kept accruing paid time, silently, for as long as the nesting had been in place. Fixed with the same domain-command split already used in the same module by `_record_hall_pass_log_impl` / `record_hall_pass_log`: the body is now the plain Productivity domain command `_record_attendance_session_impl` (no envelope), and `record_attendance_session` is a thin `@requires_feat_context("FEAT-PROD-001")` entry that delegates to it. Routes and other top-level ingresses keep using the FEAT entry unchanged; the enforcement job — which deliberately closes many seats under **one** envelope, which is what makes the sweep atomic — now composes the domain command directly. The swallowing handler was also narrowed: `except FEATContextError: raise` re-raises constitutional violations so a nesting defect aborts the run and surfaces instead of masquerading as per-seat noise. The other three job envelopes were audited as part of this fix and are correct: `FEAT-PROD-004` and `FEAT-STOR-002` open a fresh per-item context inside an *undecorated* function (one top-level FEAT per class/item, by design), `complete_payroll_cycle` / `expire_entitlement` / `delete_due_policy_lineages` are undecorated, and `database_maintenance_job` invokes no FEAT at all. Regression: `tests/test_admin_tenancy.py` went from 2 failed / 7 passed to **9 passed** (stash-verified against the unmodified tree); `tests/dom/prod` + `tests/dom/attendance` at 26 passed with one pre-existing hall-pass-verify failure unchanged.

- **A `users` row no longer outlives its last seat (2026-09-04)** — Closes blocker **B3**. A `User` is the auth principal *behind* a seat and has no standalone existence: once the last seat referencing it is gone, the principal must be deleted too (INV-CORE-000 §III.5, DOM-IDEN-001 §VI). Because `users` carries credential and recovery material, retaining the row is also a PII-retention violation (INV-ARC-018). The tracker recorded this as "deletes seats but leaves the parent `User`" — it was worse than that. **Both orphan sweeps were tautologies that deleted nothing at all.** `_delete_orphan_students` selected seats whose `user_id` was in the affected set and then filtered out seats whose `user_id` was in the affected set, so the result was always empty; the class-teardown sweep ran *after* deleting the class's seats, so the set it drove from was already gone. Neither had ever removed a row. Added the canonical helper `delete_orphaned_users(user_ids)` in `app/utils/student_deletion.py`: it retains only ids with no `Seat` in any class and no owned `ClassEconomy` — teacher principals are exempt because they are terminal-destroyed through `FEAT-IDEN-007`, which tears their classes down first — then clears the references that would otherwise block the delete (`attendance_sessions` rows and `recovery_requests` are removed, since the former is `ON DELETE SET NULL` against a `NOT NULL` column; `ledger_transaction.user_id`, `issues.sysadmin_id`, and `policy_transitions.created_by` are nulled) and deletes the `users` rows. Credential material follows by FK cascade. All three call paths now route through the one helper — `hard_delete_student_if_orphaned`, `remove_student_from_teacher_scope`, and the class teardown inside `_destroy_class_scope_rows` — and the acting principal is excluded from the sweep at both destruction sites, so a teacher survives destroying one of their own classes and is removed exactly once, terminally, by FEAT-IDEN-007. New `tests/dom/identity/test_orphaned_user_deletion.py` (6 passed) covers the last-seat delete, preservation of a principal still seated in another class, teacher exemption, the production detach path (the seat survives as an unclaimed roster slot; the principal does not), class destruction, and credential removal. `tests/dom/class` shows the same 7 pre-existing failures before and after the change.

- **Destruction authority is canonical-context only; both teacher destruction surfaces repaired (2026-09-04)** — Two distinct defects. **(1) Class destruction selected its target from a public alias.** `POST /admin/join-code/delete` read `join_code` out of the request body and destroyed whatever class that alias resolved to — a cross-tenant isolation violation (INV-CORE-000 §III.1: `class_id` is canonical, `join_code` is a *public* alias). It also honored a `confirm_join_code` shortcut that skipped `_validate_destruction_gate` entirely, meaning anyone holding the join code — which every student in the class has — could echo it back and bypass the 30-second countdown, typed-phrase, and 10-second press-and-hold gate. The route now resolves the target **exclusively** from `g.canonical_context.class_id`, verifies ownership through `verify_teacher_owns_class`, enforces the gate unconditionally, and clears `User.last_active_class_id`/`last_active_seat_id` so the destroyed scope cannot survive as a canonical pointer (INV-ARC-012 §V). A supplied `join_code` is now inert noise. **(2) Account destruction was dead on arrival and leaked an internal identifier.** `GET /admin/account-delete` rendered its confirmation phrase from `get_display_username()`, which returns `f"user_{User.id}"` — the internal primary key, presented to the user as an identity. Worse, the gate JavaScript bound to `getElementById('deletion-request-form')` while the form was rendered as `account-delete-form`; the null lookup threw, killing the whole `DOMContentLoaded` handler, so the modal never fired and the page posted natively with an empty `gate_phrase` (the reported "phrase doesn't match" symptom). The phrase is now produced by the lawful Identity display read (`build_identity_profile_view` → `identity_profiles`) as `DELETE <NAME>'S ACCOUNT`, server-rendered into `data-confirmation-phrase` and never reconstructed client-side, with a non-identity `DELETE MY ACCOUNT` fallback when no display identity resolves. **Deletion authority in both flows comes from the canonical context alone** — display names and join codes are presentation only and never participate in lookup, authorization, or target resolution. Two latent bugs surfaced and were fixed underneath: `_hard_delete_teacher_account_scope` was passed an `int` where it expects a canonical context (a signature change on 2026-07-11 updated the argument *name* but not its type), so every teacher account deletion since then raised `ValueError` into a broad `except` and flashed a generic error; and `_delete_teacher_residual_ownership_rows` called `.delete()` on a joined query, which SQLAlchemy forbids — rewritten on the subquery pattern used elsewhere in that family. Teacher account destruction had no lawful mutation envelope at all — it ran bare `db.session.delete()`, which the FEAT guard rejects — so it is now built to canonical shape as **`FEAT-IDEN-007` (Teacher Account Destruction, HIGH)**, with a registry entry and a contract at `docs/FEATURE-EXECUTION/FEAT-IDEN-007_TEACHER_ACCOUNT_DESTRUCTION.md`. Because exactly one FEAT executes per request (INV-ARC-000 §VIII.2, INV-ARC-021 §V.2), the class-destruction body was split into a plain domain command (`_destroy_class_scope_rows`) with `_hard_delete_class_scope` retained as the thin `FEAT-CLASS-001` public entry for single-class destruction; `_hard_delete_teacher_account_scope` is now the `@requires_feat_context("FEAT-IDEN-007")` entry that **composes that domain command** rather than executing FEAT-CLASS-001 — the same "compose domain commands, not other FEATs" correction applied to the Obligations cluster in part 1 of the execution-boundary remediation. One envelope now spans the whole teardown, which is what makes it atomic: a failure inside any class teardown rolls back the entire account deletion instead of leaving a half-destroyed principal. Built the class-destruction UI, which did not previously exist anywhere in `templates/` or `static/`, reusing the existing gate modal, its `aria-live` feedback region, and the established interaction contract. New `tests/dom/identity/test_destruction_authority_boundary.py` (21 passed) pins the boundary: account deletion targets only the canonical `user_id`; a foreign display name deletes nothing; the phrase never contains `users.id`; the gate is bound to the form that actually exists; class deletion targets only `g.canonical_context.class_id`; a supplied or foreign `join_code` can neither select nor switch the target; the `confirm_join_code` bypass is gone; missing/short/incorrect gate evidence fails closed on both surfaces; a duplicate `display_name` on another class is inert; and the destroyed class's canonical pointer is cleared.

- **Accessibility/usability review continuation (2026-09-03)** — Added keyboard-safe sortable-table headers with `aria-sort`, named button semantics for Attendance and Hall Pass pagination plus Payroll template actions, shared post-render icon suppression, textual data equivalent for the student savings chart, accessible character counters, standalone offline/error orientation, and standard dashboard markup. These changes preserve the canonical feature workflows while reducing ambiguity and assistive-technology noise. Validation is documented in `docs/ACCESSIBILITY_REVIEW_2026-09-03.md`.

- **Student Store feedback is assistive-technology friendly (2026-09-03)** — Replaced browser alerts for purchase and item-use validation/server outcomes with focused in-modal live regions. Students now hear actionable messages for missing passphrases/PINs, invalid quantities, failed requests, and successful actions while the existing Store purchase and entitlement-use FEAT paths remain unchanged.

- **Hall Pass feedback is keyboard- and screen-reader usable (2026-09-03)** — Replaced browser `alert()` error and save messages in the teacher Hall Pass workflow with an in-page assertive status region that receives focus and explains the next action. Approval/rejection, leave/return, queue-limit validation, settings saves, and verification-link regeneration now report failures without relying on a browser dialog; successful state updates remain unchanged.

- **Accessibility and public-page icon rendering remediation (2026-09-03)** — Replaced external Google font dependencies on the public learn-more, district, terms, privacy, and progress pages with the same-origin vendored font stylesheet. Added the unprefixed Material Symbols ligature setting so Chromium and other modern browsers render icon glyphs instead of exposing names such as `local_atm` and `arrow_circle_left` as visible text. The shared teacher/student/system-admin shells now expose distinct navigation landmarks, current-page state, and menu relationships. Added accessible names to high-use recovery, claims, attendance, payroll, rent, support, issue, and log controls; converted the documentation timeline’s legacy clickable headers into keyboard-operable disclosures; and replaced Economy Features save alerts with an inline live status. Validation: `42 passed, 68 skipped` across the mapped template/public accessibility checks; authenticated rendered journeys remain pending live class/session fixtures.

- **FEAT execution-boundary remediation, part 1 — insurance policy creation unblocked + the Obligations orchestration cluster stops executing other FEATs (2026-09-02)** — Creating an insurance policy through the admin route crashed with `FEATContextError: Illegal nested FEAT context` because FEAT-CLASS-003 (`configure_insurance_definition`) executed FEAT-POL-001 (`execute_store_insurance_definition`) — a FEAT-to-FEAT call, forbidden by INV-ARC-000 §VIII.2 ("exactly one command path per request") and INV-ARC-021 §V.2 (the FEAT is the sole composition layer, within one execution path); mutation belongs in explicit **domain commands** (INV-ARC-006). The guard was correctly catching a real architectural violation; the tests missed it only because they passed `corr_`-prefixed correlation ids that coincidentally normalized to match, while the route's unprefixed id tripped the string comparison. Fix: FEAT-CLASS-003 now invokes the POL **domain commands** (`insurance_definition_service.create_insurance_definition` / `set_availability`) directly, never the FEAT-POL-001 executor (admin creation route regression added: `tests/test_admin_insurance_creation_route.py`). A repo-wide audit then showed the pattern is systemic; part 1 remediates the **Obligations orchestration cluster**: `FEAT-OBL-004` (insurance purchase), `rent_payment`, `reconcile_rent`, and the `nsf_fee` helper now compose the Obligations **domain commands** (`establish_bill_cycle` / `advance_bill_cycle` / `assess_obligation` / `satisfy_obligation` plain functions) within their single FEAT context instead of calling `execute_*` FEAT wrappers; the decorative `context=FEATContext(...)` params on those wrappers are cleaned to `context=None`. Business semantics are unchanged (Obligations/Insurance/Rent/NSF regressions green: 45 + 22 + 11 passed across runs). **Deferred to part 2** (a larger, system-wide effort touching the canonical monetary workflow and payroll): removing the FEAT-CORE-000 §V.2 "Core-FEAT" carve-out, refactoring `FEAT-STOR-001/003 → FEAT-LED-000` and `→ FEAT-PROD-002/003` (surfaced by a structural AST check), hardening `FEATContext` to reject any nested FEAT entry regardless of feat_name/correlation, and adding the structural CI + boundary tests — none of which can land until those remaining callers are clean.

### Added

- **`REF-API-001` replaces the v1 API reference, and three defects it found are fixed (2026-09-05)** — `ARCHITECTURE/OPERATIONS/ARC-OPS-005_Api_Reference` was quarantined with the rest of the v1 archive and had no v2 successor, so the operator docs carried a dangling link and nobody could answer "what does this application expose over HTTP." `docs/REFERENCE/REF-API-001_HTTP_INTERFACE_REFERENCE.md` is that successor: an inventory of the 63 JSON-returning endpoints among the application's 198 registered rules, each grouped by capability and dispositioned Live / Operational / Stale / Broken / Prohibited by cross-referencing every path against `templates/` and `static/js/`. The doc is deliberately descriptive rather than normative — there is no public API, no versioning, and the `/api` prefix is not a boundary (16 of the 63 live outside it) — and §X records how to regenerate it, including the two false results a naive decorator scan produces. Building it surfaced three defects, all now fixed. **`/debug/filters` and `/debug/admin-db-test`** were registered on `main_bp` in every environment with no auth decorator, no environment gate, and no rate limit beyond the global default; verified against a running instance with credentials omitted, the first returned **200** and the complete Jinja filter list, and the second exists solely to query the users table and report a teacher count. Neither had a caller; both are deleted. **`/admin/payroll/rewards/add`** was a route decorator whose function body had been removed, so it silently bound to the *next* function in the file and advertised a capability that raised `TypeError` before reaching any application logic — the rule supplies no `transaction_id` while `void_payroll_transaction` requires one. Nothing in the suite noticed, because a broken route with no caller has nothing to fail. The orphaned decorator is deleted. Removing `debug_admin_db_test` then exposed a defect it had been sharing with **`/health/deep`**: both referenced `UserRole`, which `main.py` never imported, so the deep health probe's teachers-table check raised `NameError` rather than reporting status (and the debug endpoint answered 500 rather than leaking the count). The import was added. New `tests/test_route_registration_contract.py` pins all three: every rule must supply the arguments its view requires, no rule may pass an argument its view cannot accept, and the two debug paths must not appear in the URL map. The first two checks are generic over the whole route table, so the next deleted function body fails at test time instead of at request time.

- **The in-app user guide (2026-09-04)** — Teachers and students now have a complete support site at `/docs`, served by the application under the product's own theme rather than left in the v1 archive. The v1 user guide, feature deep dives, and diagnostics pages were rewritten against the v2 UI and vocabulary, and new guides were written for every surface that had none (attendance and tap, hall passes, insurance, the store, payroll, rent and bills, savings interest, account recovery, class setup, and the public hall-pass verification page). The landing page and the article reader were rebuilt on the marketing hero (`landing.html`/`learnmore.html`) with a `district.html`-style table of contents, and the reader is now a fixed-viewport shell whose article and TOC columns scroll independently so the page itself never overflows on mobile. Contextual help is wired throughout the app: `templates/macros/help.html` renders Guide buttons from a `help_doc_map` in each layout, keyed on the page name with a `request.endpoint` fallback, and `docs_url_for` keeps a signed-in user inside the application instead of bouncing them to the published site and costing them their session. The audience switch that used to offer "Tech" alongside "User" is now visible only to system administrators — the developer-namespaced docs (`INV-*`, `DOM-*`, `FEAT-*`, `SPEC-*`, `SOP-*`) are not written for teachers, and the operator-only links that still pointed at v1-archive paths were repointed at their v2 successors. `tests/test_docs_help_links.py` pins three contracts: every `help_doc_map` entry resolves to a real file, every doc path referenced anywhere in `templates/` resolves to a real file, and the layouts actually render a working help link on representative teacher and student pages. That middle test exists because a hand-written link in the search page's "Popular Topics" list had already gone stale without anything noticing.

- **Insurance claim form migrated off the dead integer-policy model (insurance-purchase arc, 2026-09-01)** — `GET/POST /student/insurance/claim/<policy_uuid>` replaces the old `<int:policy_id>` route (no compatibility shim — the legacy shape is deleted, not redirected). The route resolves the student's **active INSURANCE entitlement for exactly this policy, class-scoped** (`_active_insurance_entitlement_id`: matches the grant's immutable `policy_uuid`, excludes EXPIRED/REVOKED lineages) and **fails closed** if the student doesn't currently hold that coverage — even when the UUID names a real policy in the same class. `policy_uuid` is never resolved globally. The claim form is a real WTForms `InsuranceClaimForm` (replacing the `_DummyField` shell) and presents policy terms from the canonical `insurance_policies` read (no snapshot, no terms reconstructed in the template — INV-ARC-022); TRANSACTION policies pick from the student's eligible money-out transactions (filtered by the lawful `insurance_eligibility_contract` sets; the FEAT remains the eligibility authority), and submission drives **FEAT-STOR-003** `submit_insurance_claim`. Rebuilt `templates/student_file_claim.html` on the canonical shape and linked "File a claim" from each owned-coverage card. Both monetary and productivity surfaces are supported: **TRANSACTION** policies pick an eligible transaction; **PRODUCTIVITY** policies get a repeatable **date + hours + explanation** multi-day surface (JS "add another day") that posts the canonical `claimed_dates` list of `{date, hours, explanation}` (+ optional claim-wide `additional_information`), exactly the shape FEAT-STOR-003 parses — the FEAT stays the authority on class-local date eligibility, worked-hours capacity, and the two-resource payout rule (non-monetary policies, unsupported by the claim FEAT, are gated with a clear message). Tests (in `tests/test_insurance_purchase_route.py`): a covered student renders the transaction form and submits a claim FEAT-STOR-003 records; a student without coverage is redirected (fail-closed) with no claim written; the productivity surface renders its multi-date rows and its submission is parsed and adjudicated by FEAT-STOR-003 (approval depends on the student's work records — covered end-to-end in `test_insurance_claim_feat.py`).

- **Students can buy insurance again — the marketplace/purchase routes are wired to FEAT-OBL-004 (insurance-purchase arc slice E, 2026-09-01)** — The student insurance surface, dead since it was left on the retired `PolicyVersion(domain="insurance")` model, is rebuilt on the canonical model end-to-end. `GET /student/insurance` now lists the class's **`IN_USE` `insurance_policies`** (title, type, premium/cadence, reimbursement %, payout multiple, claim window) with an owned-coverage indicator, plus the student's active coverage (resolved from `EntitlementEvent` history through the immutable policy) and their claims. `POST /student/insurance/purchase/<policy_uuid>` (was `<int:policy_id>` against `PolicyVersion`) drives **`execute_purchase_insurance`** (FEAT-OBL-004): a purchase assesses + satisfies the first premium and grants the `INSURANCE` entitlement atomically, with friendly flash messages for `POLICY_ALREADY_HELD` / `INSUFFICIENT_FUNDS` / unavailable / not-found. A fresh per-request idempotency key means a double-submit is caught by `POLICY_ALREADY_HELD` (never a double charge) while a lawful re-purchase after cancellation starts a clean lineage. Rebuilt `templates/student_insurance_marketplace.html` on the canonical shape (dropping the legacy tier/enrollment/`policy.id` machinery); the still-legacy claim-form / cancel / view-policy routes are intentionally not linked yet (their rewiring is the next sub-slices). Folded in the earlier guards that keep INSURANCE entitlements out of the *general* store item display (`student.py`) and the availability-based badge on the admin insurance page (`For sale` / `Hidden — winding down`). Tests: new `tests/test_insurance_purchase_route.py` (marketplace lists a policy and a funded student buys it end-to-end → coverage active and shown as owned; insufficient funds flashes and writes nothing); the existing `/student/insurance` schema-surface check still passes. *Known limitation:* FEAT-OBL-004 derives its correlation/ledger identifiers from the `idempotency_key`, so a very long caller-supplied key can overflow a `varchar(100)` column — the route passes a short key; bounding this inside the FEAT is a follow-up.

### Changed

- **Insurance claims resolve the immutable policy via `policy_uuid` — the frozen snapshot model is dead (insurance-purchase arc slice D, 2026-09-01)** — FEAT-STOR-003 (insurance claim lifecycle) now computes all eligibility and economics from the **immutable `insurance_policies` definition**, resolved through the GRANTED entitlement's `policy_uuid` (`_resolve_claim_policy`), instead of reading a `frozen_contract` payload snapshot. Because a policy edit mints a *new* `policy_uuid`, the exact row an entitlement references cannot change after purchase — it *is* the frozen contract, same non-retroactivity guarantee, with **no duplicated policy terms in the entitlement payload** (INV-ARC-009, DOM-STORE-001 §VII.A). The entitlement proves acquisition; the policy provides the terms. **Strict, no compatibility fallback** (per the pre-release directive): a claimed grant that carries no `policy_uuid`, or a `policy_uuid` that does not resolve within the claim's class, **fails closed** (`POLICY_UNRESOLVABLE`) — there is no `if policy_uuid … else frozen_contract` third path. With the last runtime reader gone, **deleted** the frozen-contract machinery entirely: `app/services/frozen_insurance_contract.py` (the `parse_frozen_contract`/`get_frozen_insurance_contract` reader + `FrozenInsuranceContract` model) and `app/services/insurance_contract_freeze.py` (the `build_frozen_contract`/`build_purchase_metadata` writer). Reworked the claim tests to create real immutable `insurance_policies` rows (via `create_insurance_definition`) and grant entitlements carrying only `{policy_uuid}`, replacing the synthetic frozen payloads; deleted `tests/test_insurance_claim_freeze_read.py` (it tested the now-deleted reader). Added fail-closed tests for missing and wrong-class `policy_uuid`. FEAT-STOR-003 doc → v1.2 (claim-time authority is the immutable policy row). Claim FEAT green (38 + 2 new fail-closed = 40 passed); no runtime claim dependency on frozen insurance payloads remains — the old snapshot system is genuinely dead, not merely unused.

### Removed

- **Insurance removed from the Store purchase path — the loose thread is cut (insurance-purchase arc slice C, 2026-09-01)** — Now that FEAT-OBL-004 owns insurance acquisition (slice B), the misbuilt store-based insurance purchase is deleted. `execute_store_purchase` (FEAT-STOR-001) is StoreProduct-only again: its entire INSURANCE branch is gone — the `insurance_policies` resolution, the availability re-check, the premium reconciliation, the `build_frozen_contract`/`build_purchase_metadata` snapshot, and the `frozen_contract`/`purchase_metadata`/`insurance_policy_uuid` payload writes — replaced by a single guard that rejects any INSURANCE-typed policy (`INSURANCE_NOT_PURCHASABLE_VIA_STORE`, directing to FEAT-OBL-004). Deleted `app/feats/store_publication_feat.py` (FEAT-STOR-007 "Publish Insurance Product") and its registry entry — the StoreProduct wrapper for insurance no longer exists, so nothing publishes one. Deleted the tests that exercised the misbuild: `tests/test_insurance_purchase_freeze_feat.py` (StoreProduct-based purchase — its behavioral assertions were ported to FEAT-OBL-004 in slice B per INV-ARC-017) and `tests/test_insurance_publication_feat.py`. Amended FEAT-STOR-001 to v3.1 (§II narrowed to "sole writer of PURCHASE grants for actual Store products"; insurance out of scope; stale insurance error codes replaced). Deliberately **kept** the frozen-contract model (`insurance_contract_freeze` / `frozen_insurance_contract`) — the claim path still reads it, and it disappears with the claim rework in slice D. Regression green: general store purchase + claim freeze-read + FEAT-OBL-004 (25 passed); app boots (199 routes) with no dangling references.

### Added

- **FEAT-OBL-004 Insurance Policy Purchase / Enrollment (insurance-purchase arc slice B, 2026-09-01)** — New dedicated insurance-purchase FEAT (`app/feats/purchase_insurance_feat.py::execute_purchase_insurance`) that resolves the class-scoped immutable `insurance_policies` definition and orchestrates the acquisition as an **Obligations** action — NOT a branch of `execute_store_purchase`, and with **no `StoreProduct`** anywhere in the path. Atomic spine under one FEAT transaction: resolve `policy_uuid` under `class_id` → verify `IN_USE` → reject same-policy concurrent coverage (`POLICY_ALREADY_HELD`, derived from entitlement history via new `entitlement_read_service.has_active_insurance_coverage`) → **`establish_bill_cycle`** genesis (cycle 1, records the next recurring-premium boundary from the policy charge cadence via canonical temporal evaluation) → assess `INSURANCE_PREMIUM` for cycle 1 → satisfy it through the canonical Ledger path (`create_pending_transaction_idempotent` + `execute_satisfy_obligation_payment`) → grant the `INSURANCE`/`PURCHASE` entitlement (new `entitlement_service.grant_insurance_entitlement`, which references `policy_uuid` in the event payload and snapshots no policy terms — DOM-STORE-001 §VII.A) → commit. `policy_uuid` is the frozen contract; terms are retrieved later by resolving the immutable row, never copied forward. Idempotency is command-owned: `correlation_id` embeds the `idempotency_key`, so a same-command replay returns the original grant (`already_enrolled`) while a different command that finds active coverage is rejected `POLICY_ALREADY_HELD`. Registered `FEAT-OBL-004` (HIGH blast radius) in the FEAT registry. **The FEAT is built and tested but intentionally unwired** — the old Store-based insurance purchase path is left fully intact (its removal is slice C; route wiring is slice E). Tests (`tests/test_insurance_purchase_feat.py`, 7 passed): the centerpiece is transaction-boundary behavior — insufficient funds / unavailable policy / policy-not-found write **nothing**; a successful purchase leaves **exactly one** cycle-1 assessment, satisfaction, grant, bill cycle, and premium debit; a same-key retry adds no duplicates; a different key after success is `POLICY_ALREADY_HELD`; and an injected failure during the grant (after the premium is charged) **rolls the entire transaction back including the Ledger post** — proving atomicity is real, not prose.

- **Bill-cycle genesis is now an explicit Obligations command (DOM-OBL-001, insurance-purchase arc slice A, 2026-09-01)** — Separated bill-cycle **genesis** (`nothing → cycle 1`) from **advancement** (`cycle N → cycle N+1`), which had been silently conflated: `reconcile_rent` created its first cycle via `execute_advance_bill_cycle(cycle_number=1)` even though FEAT-OBL-002's contract defines "advance" as successor-only. New `app/feats/establish_bill_cycle_feat.py::establish_bill_cycle` / `execute_establish_bill_cycle` establishes cycle 1 with a hard precondition that **no prior cycle exists** for the lineage — a second genesis raises `BillCycleLifecycleError` regardless of idempotency key (idempotency guards retries, not a second cycle 1). `cycle_number = 1` is intrinsic, never caller-selected. Genesis carries no new FEAT-registry number; it runs under the shared `FEAT-OBL-002` bill-cycle authority (the genesis-vs-advancement distinction lives in the command contracts). Introduced `BillCycleLifecycleError` in `obligations_service`. This is slice A of the FEAT-OBL-004 insurance-purchase arc (see `docs/FEATURE-EXECUTION/FEAT-OBL-004_INSURANCE_POLICY_PURCHASE.md`); it provides the lawful genesis primitive that insurance enrollment needs before any recurring premium can be assessed.

### Changed

- **Bill-cycle advancement is now advancement-only (FEAT-OBL-002 v1.1, 2026-09-01)** — `advance_bill_cycle` now **requires an existing current cycle** (raises `BillCycleLifecycleError` if none — advancement is not genesis) and **derives-and-verifies** the strict successor number (`current + 1`), rejecting any non-sequential value; the caller-supplied `cycle_number` serves only as the replay/idempotency key and is checked against the derived successor. Migrated `reconcile_rent_feat` genesis from `execute_advance_bill_cycle(cycle_number=1)` to `execute_establish_bill_cycle`, so rent no longer relies on the old contract overloading. Amended the governing docs in the same change (INV-ARC-017): FEAT-OBL-002 spec (§I/§IV/§V, advancement-only + derived successor) and DOM-OBL-001 (§V.7 genesis/advancement/termination lifecycle; `bill_cycles` generalized from rent-only to any continuing obligation-producing relationship, distinguished by `internal_ref`/`obligation_type`). Tests: new `tests/test_bill_cycle_genesis.py` (genesis produces cycle 1; second genesis fails even as a fresh invocation; advance rejects genesis; advance rejects non-sequential successor; sequential advance succeeds); updated `tests/dom/obligations/test_obligations_domain.py` to the split lifecycle. Genesis + obligations (15 passed) and rent/obligation regression (32 passed) green.

- **Interpretation page made teacher-legible (DOM-ITR-001, slice §8.4c-follow-up, 2026-09-01)** — Reworked the ITR presentation content so a teacher can *interpret* the page rather than re-analyze it, and renamed the surface to **Interpretation** (route `/admin/interpretation/`, nav label + `insights` icon). Each of the seven sections now carries a **"How to read this"** explanation (a calm callout, not an alert) stating both what the section shows and how to make sense of it — e.g. resources: "money left over at the end, not money earned … watch the 'at or below zero' count."
